5.7. A basic circuit: the binary adder
Numbers are represented in the binary system for computer use. The
computer, via its processing unit, performs calculations on these numbers.
The simplest operation is addition. Addition in the binary system is carried
out similarly to addition in the decimal system. For example, to perform the
decimal addition 24 + 57 = 81, it can be translated into the binary system by
adding 11000 (corresponding to 24 in binary) with 111001 (corresponding

to 57 in binary), resulting in 1010001 (corresponding to 81 in binary), as
shown in Figure 5.56.
In binary arithmetic, as in decimal arithmetic, the addition of two numbers
can generate carries. In the binary system, there are only two possible
digits, 0 and 1, so adding two digits can give a sum of 0, 1, 10 (equivalent
to 2 in decimal) or 11 (equivalent to 3 in decimal) if a carry is generated.
Figure 5.56. Binary addition
In the given example, the sum of 24 and 57 in decimal is 81. In binary, this
corresponds to the addition of 11000 and 111001, which gives 1010001.
The schema shows two carries generated when adding the columns of bits
with weights 2 and 4. These carries are highlighted in red in the schema of
Figure 5.56.
A half-adder is an elementary component that performs column-wise
addition, similar to “manual” addition. This component takes two bits x and
y as inputs and produces a sum S and a possible carry R as outputs. This
component must follow the addition table shown in Figure 5.57.
Figure 5.57. Truth tables for S and R
Two Boolean functions S and R must be found that provide the correct
results. These functions are not difficult to find:
These functions were illustrated in a previous example, and the logic circuit
corresponding to this half-adder is shown in Figure 5.58. Specifically, S = x

⊕ y, where ⊕ denotes exclusive OR. Therefore, an XOR gate is used to
obtain S, and an AND gate is used to obtain R.
Figure 5.58. Logic circuit for S and R
A full adder is a combinatorial logic circuit used for column-wise additions,
considering the carry from the previous step. Unlike the half-adder, the full
adder takes three inputs: two bits to be added (x and y) and a carry input (c).
The outputs are a sum S and a carry-out R for the next step. To construct a
full adder, two half-adders can be combined with an OR logic gate. The
Boolean functions for the result S and the carry R are as follows:
where the symbol “⊕” designates the XOR logic gate, the symbol “.”
Designates the AND logic gate and the symbol “+” designates the OR logic
gate. The logic circuit corresponding to the full adder is given in Figure
5.59, where rn−1 plays the role of c in the previous equations.
Several full adders can be used in parallel to add two binary numbers
represented by several bits, for example, a 4-bit number X and a 4-bit
number Y. The bits of the numbers X and Y are denoted as X0, X1, X2, X3
for X and Y0, Y1, Y2, Y3 for Y, where index 0 indicates the lowest weight
and index 3 the highest weight. Similarly, the bits of the sum S are also
denoted as S0, S1, S2, S3. The parallel connection is shown in Figure 5.60.

6
Arithmetic
CONCEPTS COVERED IN THIS CHAPTER.–
Arithmetic is often presented as the science of numbers and the study of
their properties. The properties, especially those of integers, form the
basis of encryption methods used to make information indecipherable
by third parties. Methods of encrypting information will be explored in
a future chapter, so it is important to first recall the basic notions of
arithmetic, focusing specifically on integers.
The discussion will start with the Euclidean division, which leads to
prime numbers, a topic that has engaged many mathematicians for
centuries.
The discussion will also cover congruences, defined by a special
operation called modulo, with an example of their use involving elliptic
curves.
The exploration will conclude with Bézout’s identity, theorem and
algorithm.
References: [VEL 19, BUC 98, MUL 18, PRA 23].
6.1. Reminder about integers
Recalling some definitions and notations relating to integers:
ℕ represents the set of natural numbers, i.e. positive integers including
zero: ℕ = {0, 1, 2, 3, …, n, …}.
ℕ* represents the set of non-zero natural numbers, i.e. all natural
numbers except zero: ℕ* = {1, 2, 3, …, n, …}.

ℤ represents the set of relative integers, i.e. positive, negative and zero
integers: ℤ = {…,−n, ….,−3,−2,−1,0,1,2,3, …,n,…} (n > 0).
ℤ* represents the set of non-zero relative integers, i.e. all relative
integers except zero: ℤ* = {…, −n, …, −3, −2, −1,1,2,3, …, n,…} (n >
0).
6.2. Euclidean division
DEFINITION.–
Euclidean division in the set ℤ: let a and b be two integers such that b ≠
0. There exist two unique integers q and r such that:
where q is called the quotient of the division of a by b, and r is called
the remainder of this division.
DEFINITION.–
Euclidean division in the set ℕ: let a and b be two natural integers such
that b is different from 0. There exist two unique integers q and r such
that:
where q is called the quotient of the division of a by b, and r is called
the remainder of this division.

DEFINITION.–
If r = 0, then a = b × q, indicating that b divides a. In this case, a is a
multiple of b (i.e. there exists a natural number q such that a = b × q),
and b is a divisor of a (i.e. there exists a natural number k such that a =
k × q).
The quotient q is also a multiple of b and k is also a multiple of a. Let us
return to the general case of the Euclidean division and demonstrate that q
and r indeed exist and are unique.
1) Existence
Let us start by recalling two results known as Archimedes’ properties:
PROPERTY 1.–
(Archimedes) Let a ∈ ℕ, b ∈ ℕ*. Then, n ∈ ℕ exists such that n × b ≥
a.
Indeed, if a = 0, take n = 1; if a ≠ 0, take n = a.
PROPERTY 2.–
(Archimedes) Let a ∈ ℕ, b ∈ ℕ*. Then, there exists q ∈ ℕ such that b
× q ≤ a < b × (q + 1).
Either E = {n ∈ ℕ | n × b > a}. According to Archimedes’ first property,
since a + 1 ∈ ℕ, there exists m such that m × b ≥ a + 1, which shows that E
is not empty. Moreover, a is a lower bound of E, which means that there
exists a smaller p of E such that p − 1 does not belong to E. Therefore, (p −
1) × b ≤ a < p × b, which can be written by positing q = p − 1: b × q ≤ a < b
× q + b, or equivalently 0 ≤ a − b × q < b which proves property 2.
If r = a − b × q then a = b × q + r and 0 ≤ r < b, which demonstrates the
existence in the case of N.
Now, let us move on to ℤ. Four cases can be considered:

When a > 0 and b > 0: the division is straightforward, as it is the same
as within ℕ.
When a > 0 and b < 0: in ℕ, a = (−b) × q + r with 0 ≤ r < −b. The
solution pair is (−q, r).
When a < 0 and b > 0: in ℕ, −a = b × q + r with 0 ℕ r < b. Therefore,
a = (−b) × q − r = b × (−q − 1) + (b − r). For r = 0, the solution pair is
(−q, 0). For r ≠ 0, the solution pair is (−q − 1, b − r).
When a < 0 and b < 0: in ℕ, −a = (−b) × q + r with 0 ≤ r < −b.
Therefore, a = b × q − r and a = b × (q + 1) − b − r. If r = 0, the
solution pair is (−q, 0). If r ≠ 0, the solution pair is (q + 1, −r − b).
Therefore, there is always a solution (q, r) in all cases for ℕ and for ℤ.
2) Uniqueness
Let us use proof by contradiction by assuming the existence of two different
pairs (q, r) and (q′, r′) such that a = b × q + r = b × q′ + r′ with 0 ≤ r, r′ < b.
Figure 6.1. |r − r′| < b
Then, it follows that b × q − b × q′ = r′ − r, or b × (q − q′) = r′ − r which
implies that b divides r′ − r. Since 0 ≤ r, r′ < b, |r′ − r| < |b| holds. Thus, r′ −
r must equal 0, leading to q = q′ and r = r′. Therefore, the pair (q, r) is
unique.
Therefore, this negates the initial hypothesis, conclusively proving the
uniqueness of the pair (q, r).
6.3. Divisibility and prime numbers
In the case where the remainder of the Euclidean division is equal to 0, the
relationship simplifies to a = b × q. This expression establishes a

relationship in ℕ* (the set of positive natural numbers) between a and b,
which can be expressed by b divides a, denoted as b | a.
The properties of the relation “|” can be summarized as follows:
Reflexivity: a | a since a = 1 × a.
Antisymmetry: b | a ⇏ a | b. If b | a, then a = b × q. However, a | b
would imply b = a × q′, which leads to a = a × q × q′. This equality
holds only if q × q′ = 1, implying q = q′ = 1 and a = b, contradicting a
≠ b (the initial assumption).
Transitivity: if c | b and b | a then c | a. Given c | b and b | a, b = c × q
and a = b × q′. Thus, a = c × q × q′ demonstrating c | a.
With these three properties, the relationship “|” forms an order relationship
in ℕ*. Additionally:
If c | a and c | b, then c | (l × a + m × b) for l, m ∈ ℤ. In particular, c |
(a + b) and c | (a − b).
DEFINITION.–
A number x ∈ ℕ*, greater than 1, is prime if it only has 1 and itself as
divisors.
Prime numbers indeed play an important role in the encryption of digital
information, a topic that will be explored further.
Let us cite Euclid’s definition of prime numbers: a prime number is a
number which has exactly 2 divisors. All integers admit 1 as a divisor; any
non-zero integer has itself as a divisor. Therefore:
0 is not prime because it has infinitely many divisors;
1 is not prime because it has only one divisor: itself;
any integer other than 0 and 1 has at least 2 divisors (1 and itself). It
may have more divisors, but if it does not, it is prime.

The first 10 prime numbers are 2, 3, 5; 7; 11, 13, 17, 19, 23.
Prime numbers have many properties, some of which we examine.
THEOREM 6.1.–
If an integer is strictly greater than 1, then its smallest divisor strictly
greater than 1 is a prime number.
The proof of the above theorem is done by reasoning through contradiction.
Let a be an integer greater than 1. It has at least two divisors: itself and 1. It
may have other divisors as well, but let ft b denote the smallest divisor
greater than 1.
Assume, for contradiction, that b is not a prime number, meaning it has a
divisor c other than 1 and itself, and c < b. Therefore, c divides b. Since b
divides a, by transitivity, c also divides a. However, this contradicts the
assumption that b is the smallest divisor of a.
Therefore, b cannot be non-prime and must be a prime number. Hence, the
smallest divisor of any integer greater than 1 is a prime number.
THEOREM 6.2.–
Any number in ℕ*, greater than or equal to 2, is either a prime number
or a product of prime numbers.
Let us prove this theorem by mathematical induction:
Base case: the proposition is true for number 2, which is a prime
number.
Inductive step: assume that the proposition is true for all numbers from
2 to n. That is, each integer k (where 2 ≤ k ≤ n) is either a prime
number or a product of prime numbers.
To prove: show that the proposition holds for n + 1.

According to theorem 6.1, n + 1 has a prime divisor p, which is the smallest
divisor of n + 1. Consider 
. Let us form the integer 
, which is
less than n + 1 since p is greater than 1. Thus, 
is between 2 and n.
By inductive hypothesis, 
 is either a prime number or a product of
prime numbers. Therefore, n + 1 itself is either prime (if 
 = 1) or a
product of p and 
, where both p and 
are prime numbers (by
the induction assumption).
Thus, the proposition is true for n + 1.
Therefore, mathematical induction, the proposition is true for all integers
ℕ* greater than or equal to 2.
Theorem 6.2 states that an integer can be decomposed into a product of
prime numbers of the form n = pa × qb × … × rc where p, q, …, r are prime
numbers. For example, the decomposition of 245 000 is 245 000 = 23 × 54 ×
72.
We may wonder how many prime numbers are there in the set ℕ*, which
has infinite cardinality.
THEOREM 6.3.–
There are countably infinitely many prime numbers.
Let us consider a sequence Sk of prime numbers p1, p2, p3, …, pk and form
the number m = p1 × p2 × p3 × … × pk+1. According to theorem 6.1, m has a
smallest prime divisor q strictly greater than 1. This prime number q is
different from the numbers in the sequence Sk.
If q were not different from the primes p1, p2, …, pk, then q would divide
both m and p1 × p2 × p3 × … × pk. Consequently, q would also divide their
difference, which is 1. However, since q > 1, this scenario contradicts the

property that only 1 and itself can divide 1. Therefore, q must indeed be a
prime number distinct from p1, p2, …, pk.
Therefore, an additional prime number is obtained that can be added to the
sequence Sk. This construction can be repeated indefinitely, showing that
the sequence Sk grows continuously, indicating that prime numbers form a
countably infinite set. Eratosthenes proposed a method for finding all prime
numbers less than a given integer N > 1: the sieve of Eratosthenes.
The sieve of Eratosthenes method consists of the following steps:
1. Create a list of all integers from 2 to N (excluding 1, which is not
prime).
2. Start with the first number in the list, which is 2, and mark it as prime.
3. Eliminate from the list all multiples of this prime number (2) by
marking them as non-prime.
4. Move to the next unmarked number in the list (3), which will be the
next prime number, and repeat step 3.
5. Continue this process until all numbers in the list are processed.
At the end of the method, the only unmarked numbers in the list will be
prime numbers less than N. The sieve of the Eratosthenes algorithm
efficiently identifies prime numbers up to a specified limit N, although its
computational complexity increases with larger values of N. For very large
values of N, more advanced methods may be employed to find prime
numbers.
The main steps of the sieve of the Eratosthenes algorithm are as follows:
L = an array of Booleans initialized to the value 1 (True) and 
of size N.
Set L[1] = 0 (False)
For i = 2 to N
    If L[i] = 1
           For j = i2 to N in steps of i
           L[j] = 0
           EndFor
    EndIf

EndFor
Return L
The algorithm notes the square of i in the loop for j. Starting from i2 (i. e., i
× i) ensures that multiples of i less than i2 have already been eliminated
(determined to be non-prime). The list L maintains information about
whether numbers from 1 to N are prime or non-prime.
Theorems 6.1 and 6.2 outline the procedure to decompose a number n into
its prime factors. Begin by identifying p1, the smallest prime divisor of the
number n. Divide n by p1 to obtain m, the quotient. Repeat the process by
finding p2, the smallest prime divisor of m Continue this iterative process m
becomes a prime number (i.e. non-decomposable), which indicates that n
has been completely decomposed into its prime factors. These steps can
also be summarized as follows:
1. Identify the smallest prime divisor p1 of n.
2. Compute the quotient m = n/p1.
3. Find the smallest prime divisor p2 of m.
4. Calculate the quotient m = m/p2.
5. Repeat steps 3 and 4 until m is a prime number.
The iterative process makes it possible to decompose n into a product of
prime factors. Each prime divisor p found is a distinct prime factor of n.
Note that theorems 6.1 and 6.2 guarantee the existence of this
decomposition into prime factors for any integer n greater than 1.

EXAMPLE 6.1.–
245000 can be decomposed into its prime factors as follows:


To construct a number from prime numbers, simply multiply them together.
For instance, using prime numbers p and q, the number m is formed as m =
p × q, which typically is not a prime number itself.
However, the inverse problem of determining the prime factors p and q
starting from a given number m is much more difficult, especially when m
is a large number. Finding the prime factors of a number is a complex
problem and often requires significant computational resources in terms of
capacity and time. Even with computer assistance, effectively factoring
large numbers can be arduous. Computers have limitations in computational
capacity and may require specific methods, such as advanced factorization
algorithms, to factor very large numbers. Additionally, proving that a
number is prime is also a complex problem, particularly for large numbers.
For example, Mersenne numbers, which are of the form 2n − 1, were once
“likely” to be prime. However, not all Mersenne numbers are prime. For
instance, (211− 1) = 23 × 89 is not prime, and (267 − 1) = 193707721 ×
761838257287 is not prime (Cole - 1903). Some Mersenne numbers are
indeed prime, such as (261 − 1) (Pervouchine - 1883). The challenge lies in
proving or disproving the primality of Mersenne numbers. With older
computers, which had limitations in terms of number representation (32-bit
coding quickly reaches limits: (2257 − 1) has 257 “1s” in binary), this task
was particularly challenging.
The study and exploration of prime numbers have been, and continue to be,
subject to significant mathematical research. Some of the greatest
mathematicians have made remarkable contributions to this field, including
Eratosthenes, Euclid, Euler, Fermat, Gauss, Dirichlet, Riemann and others.
Thus, the prime numbers constitute an infinity within the infinity of
integers. There are 168 prime numbers among integers up to 1,000 (16.8%),
9,592 among integers up to 100,000 (9.6%), 33,860 among integers up to
400,000 (8.5%), 74,498 among integers up to 1,000,000 (7.8%), 5761455
among integers up to 100,000,000 (5.8%). They become less frequent
among large integers. The largest known prime number, as of 2023, is
(282589933 − 1).

6.4. GCD and LCM
The abbreviations used in the title of this section mean greater common
divisor (GCD) and least common multiple (LCM), respectively.
Given two numbers a and b belonging to ℕ* (the set of strictly positive
integers), let E be the set of their common divisors. The smallest element of
E is obviously 1, and the largest element is less than or equal to min(a, b).
This indicates that E is a finite set. There is a largest element in E, called the
GCD.
EXAMPLE 6.2.–
The numbers 28 and 36 have 1, 2 and 4 as common divisors; the GCD
is therefore 4: GCD(28,36) = 4.
DEFINITION.–
The numbers a and b are coprime if GCD(a, b) = 1
This means that two prime numbers have no common divisor other than 1.
EXAMPLE 6.3.–
The numbers 28 and 27 are coprime. Indeed, 28 = 22 × 7 and 27 = 33
have no common factor (other than 1 of course).
Euclid’s algorithm finds the GCD of two numbers iteratively. This
algorithm is based on the following property:
THEOREM 6.4.–
Let a and b be in ℕ* and r is the remainder of the Euclidean division of
a by b, then GCD(a, b) = GCD(b, r).

The Euclidean division of a by b, denoted as a = b × q + r, is a fundamental
property. If d is a divisor of both b and r, then it also divides a. Therefore, d
is a common divisor of a and of b.
Conversely, if d is a common divisor of a and b, then it divides r = a − b × q
as well. Hence, d is a common divisor of b and r.
In other words, the set of common divisors of a and b is identical to the set
of common divisors of b and r. Specifically, this equivalence implies that
GCD(a, b) = GCD(b, r).
Assuming a > b, the Euclidean algorithm proceeds by repeating applying
the division: a = p1 × b + r1, b = p2 × r1 + r2, r1 = p3 × r2 + r3, etc., until the
remainder rn is zero. The last non-zero remainder obtained in this process is
indeed the GCD of the numbers a and b.
EXAMPLE 6.4.–
a = p1 × b + r1:
1680 = 1 × 1485 + 195, with a = 1680, p1 = 1, b = 1485 and r1 = 195
b = p2 × r1 + r2:
1485 = 195 × 7 + 120, with b = 1485, p2 = 195, r1 = 7 and r2 = 120
Since the remainder has become 0, the last non-zero remainder obtained
in the Euclidean algorithm is 15, i.e. GCD(1680,1485) = 15.
Prime factor decomposition allows common factors to be identified
between two numbers. Multiplying these common factors gives the GCD.

EXAMPLE 6.5.–
1680 = 24 × 3 × 5 × 7 and 1485 = 33 × 5 × 11. The product of the
common factors is 3 × 5 = 15, which is the GCD of 1680 and 1485.
Let us look at some of the properties of GCD.
THEOREM 6.5.–
The common divisors of the numbers a and b belonging to ℕ* are all
the divisors of the GCD of a and b. In other words, the set of divisors
common to a and b is equal to the set of divisors of the GCD(a, b).
Let p = GCD(a, b). Let d be a divisor of p. Therefore, d divides both a and
b. Thus, d is a common divisor of a and b. In conclusion, every divisor of
GCD(a, b) is also a common divisor of a and b.
Conversely, let d be a common divisor of a and b. Suppose a > b. There are
two possible cases:
1. If b divides a, then GCD(a, b) = b. Therefore, b divides GCD(a, b).
2. If b does not divide a, Euclid’s algorithm (theorem 6.1) is applied to
obtain GCD(a, b) = GCD(b, r), where r is the remainder of the
division of a by b.
Continuing Euclid’s algorithm,
, where rn is
the last non-zero remainder.
In both cases, d divides each remainder ri of the iterations of Euclid’s
algorithm, including the last remainder rn, which is equal to GCD(a, b).
Thus, any common divisor of a and b also divides GCD(a, b).

THEOREM 6.6.–
a, b, c ∈ ℕ*, GCD(a × c, b × c) = c × GCD(a, b).
Euclid’s algorithm is used to prove this theorem. It proceeds as follows:
1. Define c = GCD(a, b).
2. Perform Euclidean division by c × a and c × b, yielding c × a = c × b ×
q1 + c × r1, where q1 is the quotient and c × r1 is the remainder.
3. By Euclid’s algorithm property, GCD(c × a, c × b) = GCD(c × b, c ×
r1).
4. Repeat the division c × b by c × r1, resulting in c × b = c × r1 × q2 + c
× r2.
5. Continue this process until c × rn = 0.
6. Therefore, GCD(c × rn−1, 0) = c × rn−1 which equals c.
Thus, it was demonstrated that GCD(a × c, b × c) = c × GCD(a, b) from
Euclid’s algorithm. This shows that the GCD of the numbers c × a and c × b
is equal to c multiplied by the GCD of a and b.
THEOREM 6.7.–
a, b, c, d ∈ ℕ*, the GCD(a, b) divides the GCD(a × c, b × d)
A divisor of a divides a × c and a divisor of b divides b × d. Therefore, any
common divisors of a and b also divide a × c and b × d. In particular
GCD(a, b) is a divisor of a × c and b × d.
The LCM of two natural numbers are the smallest number that is a multiple
of both of them. So,

To find the LCM of two numbers from their prime decompositions, select
each prime factor that appears in either number’s decomposition. For each
prime factor, choose the highest exponent that appears in either number’s
decomposition.
EXAMPLE 6.6.–
LCM(24, 36)?
There is a very simple relationship between the GCD and the LCM of two
natural integers a and b:
THEOREM 6.8.–
GCD(a, b)×LCM(a, b) = a × b.
This relationship is very useful because it allows the calculation of the
LCM of a number from its GCD with another number, or vice versa,
without the need to directly perform the decomposition into prime factors.
To prove this property, the analysis focuses on the exponents of prime
factors in number decompositions. Consider p as any prime number. Let g
represent the exponent of p in the prime factorization of a, and h the
exponent of p in the prime factorization of b. The exponent of p in the
prime factorization of (a × b) is therefore (g + h).
Let u = GCD(a, b). The exponent of p in the prime factorization of u is
denoted as e, where e = min(g, h). Define v = LCM(a, b). The exponent of p

in the prime factorization of v is denoted as f, where f = max(g, h).
Consequently, the exponent of p in the prime factorization of (u × v) is (e +
f).
If g ≥ h, then (e + f) = (h + g); if g ≤ h, then (e + f) = (g + h). In either
scenario, (e + f = g + h). This implies that the exponent of p remains
consistent between the prime factorization of (a × b) and (u × v). Due to the
uniqueness of prime decomposition, it follows that (a × b) = (u × v).
EXAMPLE 6.7.–
Let a = 12 and b = 18. The GCD(12,18) = 6. Using the relation,
theLCM can be calculated as follows:
So, in this example, the LCM of 12 and 18 is equal to 36.
6.5. Congruencies
Modulo division is complex but involves the use of modulus. Modulo
division is defined as follows:
DEFINITION.–
Let a and b be two integers and n be an integer greater than or equal to
2. The operation a ≡ b [n] is called a congruence of module n (read as
“a is congruent to b modulo n”). This operation means that the
Euclidean division of a by n gives a quotient q and the remainder b.
Formally, this is expressed as: a = q × n + b, and
Additionally, n divides a − b since a − b = q × n.

EXAMPLE 6.8.–
To illustrate congruence modulo n, consider the example involving the
number 28. Since 28 = 3 × 9 + 1, then 28 ≡ 1 [3] or even 28 ≡ 1 [9].
Verify that 3 divides 28 − 1 = 27 and that 9 divides 28 − 1 = 27.
THEOREM 6.9.–
For a fixed n, the congruence a ≡ b [n] is an equivalence relation in ℤ.
Recall that an equivalence relation ℛ is a map having the properties of
reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity:
For congruence, an equivalence relation a ≡ b [n] is a relationship that
satisfies the properties of reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity:
Reflexivity: a ≡ a [n] means that a can be expressed in the form a = q ×
n + a, where q = 0.
Symmetry: a ≡ b[n] means that a can be expressed in the form a = q ×
n + b. The equation can also be written as b = (−q) × n + a, which
shows that b ≡ a [n].
Transitivity: if a ≡ b [n] and b ≡ c [n], it means that a can be expressed
as a = p × n + b and b can be expressed as b = q × n + c. Substituting
the second equation into the first one, a = (p + q) × n + c is obtained,
which shows that a ≡ c [n].
Thus, the congruence relation satisfies the properties of reflexivity,
symmetry and transitivity, making it an equivalence relation.

EXAMPLE 6.9.–
If n = 3, the following three disjoint sets, called equivalence classes, can
be found:
E0 = {…, -3, 0, 3, 6, 9, …}
E1 = {…, -2, 1, 4, 7, 10, …}
E2 = {…, -1, 2, 5, 8, 11, …}
E3 = {…, 0, 3, 6, 9, 12, …} = E0
Addition and multiplication “modulo n” can be defined. Let, for example, a
≡ b[n] and a′ ≡ b′[n]. So:
Indeed, a = p × n + b and a′ = q × n + b′ therefore, a + a′ = (p + q) × n + b +
b′ or a + a′ ≡ b + b′ [n].
Likewise, a × a′ = (p × n + b)(q × n + b′) = (p × q + b × q + b′ × p)n + b × b’
or a × a′ ≡ b × b′ [n].
THEOREM 6.10.–
(Fermat’s Little Theorem): let a be an integer and n be a prime number.
1. an ≡a[n].
2. if n and a are coprime (GCD(a,n)=1, then an−1 ≡1[n].
The above theorem 6.10 was stated by Fermat who, perhaps, did not prove
it. The first known demonstrations are due to Euler and Leibnitz. It should
not be confused with Fermat’s “Great” Theorem (only proven in 1995 by
Andrew Wiles), which shows the impossibility of writing an + bn = cn with

a,b,c as positive integers and n > 2. The only valid relationship for n = 2 is
given by the Pythagorean theorem: a2 + b2 = c2.
Let us move on to the proof of the “Little” Theorem using induction. Note
that for a = 0, 0n = q × n + 0 (with q= 0), which is trivially congruent to
0[n]. Therefore, 0n = 0[n].

Finding a prime factor of a large number can be challenging. For instance,

This challenge highlights the foundation of certain encryption systems.
Given primes p and q, their product Z = p × q is straightforward to
calculate. However, from Z, determining p and q is computationally
difficult. This characteristic forms the basis of the RSA (Rivest–Shamir–
Adleman) encryption system, which will be discussed in Chapter 8.
6.6. Elliptic curves
Fermat’s little theorem is well-known, but Fermat’s “big” theorem was only
demonstrated by Andrew Wiles in 1994. He used the properties of a family

of curves called elliptic curves. The details of this demonstration are not
covered here, but the focus is on the elliptic curves themselves.
In the family of elliptic curves, consider particularly the subfamily of
equations of the form y2 = x3 + ax + b, where a and b are integers such that
4a3 + 27b2 ≠ 0. The discriminant Δ = 4a3 + 27b2 is used to characterize
these curves. It allows the determination of properties of the roots of the
cubic equation x3 + ax + b. If Δ ≠0, the equation has no multiple roots. If Δ
< 0, the equation has three distinct real roots. Conversely, if Δ > 0, the
equation has only one unique real root.
The curves in this family display symmetry with respect to the x-axis (the
horizontal axis Ox). An example is the curve represented by the equation y2
= x3 − 5x + 3, where a = −5 and b = 3. This equation has a discriminant Δ =
−257.

Figure 6.3. Example of an elliptic curve
Figure 6.4. Another example of an elliptic curve
Figure 6.3 graphically shows the curve, highlighting its characteristic shape.
This curve consists of two components. With this family, curves with a

single component can also be found, such as the equation y2 = x3 − x + 2
(with discriminant Δ = 104), shown in Figure 6.4. These (elliptic) curves
possess remarkable properties. One of these properties concerns the so-
called addition operation between the points of the curve. Consider two
points A and B on the elliptic curve. Drawing a straight line passing through
these two points generally intersects the curve at a third point D. Let C be
the symmetric point of D with respect to the abscissa axis (horizontal axis
Ox). An addition operation between points A and B is defined as A + B = C.
This addition operation on elliptic curves has properties similar to those of
number addition:
It is commutative: A + B = B + A, regardless of the order of the points.
It is associative: (A + B) + C = A + (B + C), regardless of how the
points are grouped.
When the abscissa of points A and B are equal (xA = xB), the line AB
becomes parallel to the ordinate axis (Oy axis) due to the curve’s symmetry
with respect to Ox. In this case, the ordinate of point B is the negative of the
ordinate of point A (yB = -yA), and point D (and therefore point C) can be
considered to be at infinity, indicating that the line AB intersects the curve
at infinity. This specific point C is denoted as Ω. Therefore, in this case, A +
B = Ω, where B is considered the reflexion of A across the Ox axis.
Considering the line AΩ (a vertical line), it intersects thecurve at B, and
point C coincides with A, resulting in A + Ω = A. These observations lead
us to conclude the following:
Addition on elliptic curves has a neutral element denoted as Ω (the
“infinity” point), such that A + Ω = Ω + A = A.
Each point A has an “inverse” or “opposite” denoted as A′, such that A
+ A′ = Ω, where A′ is the opposite of A. Alternatively, A′ = -A.
Note that if points A and B coincide, line AB becomes tangent to the curve,
and A + A = 2A. This property is particular when points are repeated on the
curve.

The relations allowing the coordinates of a “sum” point to be found can be
analytically derived by solving the following system of equations, which
represents the intersection of a line AB with the elliptic curve:
where the second equation represents the line AB. Given the coordinates of
points A(xA, yA) and B(xB, yB), the coefficients c and d are deduced as
follows:

and B coincide:
In the general case, solving the system leads to a third-degree equation in x.
As two points of intersection (providing two roots of the equation), and
because a cubic equation has three roots, finding the third root gives the x-
coordinate of the desired point A+B:
Thus, the coordinates of point A + B are (x, −y).
Now, considering a bit of abstraction, replace the equation of the elliptic
curve C with an equation modulo p representing an (abstract) elliptic curve
Γ, where x and y are integers:

and y satisfy the above equation. Here, all points of Γ no longer have any
relation to the previous “continuous” elliptic curves.
Let us outline the following rules of addition:
a. (xΩ + yΩ)+(xΩ, yΩ)=(xΩ, yΩ), where Ω is the “infinity” point.
b. (x,y)+ (xΩ, yΩ) = (x, y).
c. (x, y) + (x, −y) = (xΩ, yΩ) (x, −y) is thus the “opposite” point of (x, y).

The relationships observed here resemble those in the previous calculations
involving points of the elliptic curve (denoted as Γ). The main distinction is
that all operations are performed modulo a specific number. If two points
P1(x1, y1) and P2(x2, y2) are points on the elliptic curve Γ, then their sum P1
+ P2 also belongs on the curve Γ, based on the previous defined
relationships.
Moreover, this addition operation forms the basis for multiplying by an
integer n. Specifically, nP is obtained by adding P to itself n times: nP = P
+ P + …. + P (n times), where P is a point on the elliptic curve. The result
nP is likewise a point on the curve Γ.

6.7. Identity, theorem and Bézout algorithm
The following proposition is known as the Bézout identity or the Bézout–
Bachet theorem:
THEOREM 6.11.–
(Bézout identity) Let a and b be two non-zero integers. There exists a
pair (u, v) of integers such that GCD(a, b)= a × u + b × v.
Denote E as the set of non-zero natural integers of the form k × a + h × b,
where k, h ∈ ℕ and E ⊆ ℕ. Since a ∈ E, E is not empty and contains a
smallest element p such that p = a × u + b × v.
The GCD(a, b) divides both a and b; hence, it also divides p, implying that
GCD(a, b) ≤ p.
Performing the Euclidean division of a by p gives a = p × q + r with 0 ≤ r ≤
p. So,
If r ≠ 0, then r ∈ E. However, this contradicts the minimality of p in E
because r < p. Therefore, r = 0.
Thus, p divides a, and similarly, p divides both a and b, implying p ≤
GCD(a, b).
Since GCD(a, b) ≤ p and p ≤ GCD(a, b), then p = GCD(a,b) = a × u + b ×
v.
THEOREM 6.12. –
(Bézout’s theorem) a, b ∈ ℤ are relatively prime if and only if there
exist u, v ∈ ℤ such that a × u + b × v= 1.

If a and b are coprime, by definition, GCD(a, b) = 1. According to Bézout’s
identity, there exist integers u and v such that a × u + b × v = 1. Therefore, a
× u + b × v = 1.
Conversely, if there exist integers u and v such that a × u + b × v = 1, then
the GCD(a, b) divides both a and b. Therefore, it divides any linear
combination of a and b, including a × u + b × v. Since a × u + b × v = 1,
this implies that the GCD(a, b) divides 1.
Therefore, GCD(a, b) = 1 if and only if there exist integers u and v such that
a × u + b × v = 1. This establishes that a and b are relatively prime.
EXAMPLE 6.12.–
The numbers 28 and 27 are relatively prime. Integers u = 1 and v = −1
exist such that 28 × u + 27 × v = 28 − 27 = 1
Example 6.12 illustrates the use of Bézout’s algorithm to find the numbers u
and v, given the numbers a and b. This algorithm is used to compute the
GCD of two numbers and determine the Bézout coefficients. It proceeds
through a sequence of iterative steps
a, b integers
r = 0
u = 0
While r ≠
    u = u + 1
    m = au
    If b > 0
           r = m −E(m/b)n
    Otherwise
           r = m−E(m/b − 1)b
    EndIf
EndWhile
v = (1−m)/b

EXAMPLE 6.13.–
Consider the numbers a = 23 and b = 34.
Since 23 is a prime number and 34 is not a multiple of 23, the numbers
a and b are co-prime. Let us apply Bézout’s algorithm:
Performing successive steps:
Therefore, u = 3 and v = (1 − 69)/34 = −2
In fact, 3 × 23 − 2 × 34 = 1.
THEOREM 6.13.–
(Bézout’s Corollary) The equation a × x + b × y = c admits integer
solutions if and only if c is a multiple of GCD(a, b).
Suppose the equation a × x + b × y = c has a solution (x0, y0). Since GCD(a,
b) divides both a and b, it also divides their linear combination a × x0 + b ×
y0. Therefore, GCD(a, b) divides c, making c a multiple of GCD(a, b).
Conversely, suppose that c is a multiple of the GCD(a, b), say c = k ×
GCD(a, b) where k is an integer. Then, by Bézout’s identity, there exist
integers u and v such that a × u + b × v = GCD(a,b). Multiplying this
identity by k, the following result holds: k (a × u + b × v) = k × GCD(a, b) =
c. Thus, (x0, y0) = (k × u, k × v) is a solution satisfying a × x0 + b × y0 = c.

Therefore, the equation a × x + b × y = c has a solution if and only if c is a
multiple of the GCD(a, b). In such cases, (x0, y0), obtained using the
coefficients u and v of Bézout’s identity, provides a particular solution.
EXAMPLE 6.14.–
The equation 3x + 5y = 2 has integer solutions because GCD(3,5) = 1
and 2 is a multiple of 1
The equation 28x + 36y = 3 has no integer solution because
GCD(28,36) = 4 and 3 is not a multiple of 4.
THEOREM 6.14.–
(Gauss’ Theorem): for integers a, b, c, where a, b, c ∈ ℤ*, If a divides
b×c and if a and b are coprime (GCD(a,b) = 1), then a divides c.
If a divides b × c, then there exists an integer k such that k × a = b × c.
Now, suppose that a and b are coprime to each other, meaning GCD(a,b) =
1. According to Bézout’s theorem, there exist integers u and v such that a ×
u + b × v = 1. By multiplying this equality by c, the following equation
holds: c × a × u + c × b × v = c. Using the first relation (k × a = b × c) from
a previous relation, and by replacing c × b × v with k × a × v, it gives c × a
× u + k × a × v = c. Therefore, (c × u + k × v) × a = c. CConsequently, a
divides c because (c × u + k × v) is an interger.
THEOREM 6.15.–
Relationship between GCD and LCM: d = GCD(a,b) and m = LCM
(a,b)
1. There exist integers a′ and b′ such a = d × a′, b = d × b′, and
2. m × d = a × b.

7
Error Protection
CONCEPTS COVERED IN THIS CHAPTER.–
Nothing is more common than the accidental transformation of a 0 into
a 1, or vice versa, when transmitting information. The causes are
diverse. Regardless, it is essential to have ways to detect errors and, if
possible, correct them automatically. This is the subject of this chapter.
After discussing the general principles of error detection and correction
in binary sequences, linear codes are defined, where the bits of the
coded information are expressed linearly as a function of the useful
information bits and the control bits.
Current transmission methods use a variety of linear codes, including
polynomial codes. Their principle will be explained in a section
dedicated to them.
The chapter concludes with an overview of convolutional codes, which
involve temporal sequences of bits.
References: [BAD 02, LAH 95; MAC 87].
7.1. General context
It is well known that imperfections in transmission media result in noise,
which can cause a number of errors due to misinterpretation of signals. The
error rate is defined as the ratio between the number of erroneous bits and
the total number of bits transmitted, such that:

Depending on the media used, the error rate generally varies from 10−5 to
10−7. For many applications, this rate is still considered too high. Therefore,
it is necessary to provide a system for detecting and possibly correcting
errors.
Statistical studies have shown that 88% of errors are due to a single wrong
bit, while 10% of errors are due to two consecutive wrong bits. This
indicates that the main problem is the correction of “isolated” errors (an
erroneous bit occurring occasionally). However, to be more precise, an error
packet is defined according to the following convention: two successive
error bits belong to distinct error packets if the number of correct bits
separating them is greater than or equal to a fixed number N (generally N =
10, as shown in Figure 7.1).
Figure 7.1. Error packets
Figure 7.2 shows the statistical distribution of error packets as a function of
their length.

Figure 7.2. Distribution of error packets
It should be noted that less than 20% of packets have a length greater than
or equal to 3.
To protect against errors, a simple process is to first use the echoplex mode
(as shown in Figure 7.3). Such a mode consists of sending the received
message back to the transmitter. The comparison, at the transmitter level,
between the transmitted message that has been stored in memory and the
echo of the received message allows the detection of possible errors. Note
that echo transport can be prone to errors, even if the message received is
correct.
Figure 7.3. Echoplex mode
If there is a difference between the message sent and the echo of the
message received, the initial message will be retransmitted. Such a
technique is often used for small, low-speed transmissions. However, for
faster or larger transmissions, echoplex mode can become expensive and
reduce the effective throughput. An improvement involves sending two
successive blocks of information: the first block contains the information to
be transmitted, and the second one is a copy of the first block. The

verification is then performed at the receiver level. If the two blocks are
identical, it is assumed that there is no error. The receiver then sends a brief
acknowledgment signal to the transmitter. In the event of a discrepancy, the
receiver sends a non-acknowledgment signal to the transmitter, prompting it
to retransmit the information. However, such a method still involves
redundancy in transmitted information.
Figure 7.4. Encoding and decoding
A more effective solution is to use error detection codes. The principle of
such a code involves adding supplementary control information to the
useful information (see Figure 7.4). The receiver then decodes and
examines the control information to determine whether the information is
correct or erroneous. Upon detecting an error, a request to retransmit the
information is sent.
Two main categories of codes can be distinguished:
Block codes: in block codes, the control information and the useful
information form an autonomous block from the point of view of
detection and possibly correction of errors.
Convolutional or recurrent codes: these codes also operate with
blocks, but error detection and correction within a block are influenced
not only by the current block but also by preceding blocks.
Now, focusing on block codes, they are classified as systematic codes when
the useful information and the control information are grouped into separate
fields within the block, as shown in Figure 7.5.

Figure 7.5. Systematic code
Consider a set of m-bit words representing useful information, allowing for
a total of 2m distinct words. Introducing control information in the form of r
bits derived uniquely from the m useful bits, the resultant “encoded”
information contains n = m + r bits. Given the unique definition of the
control bits, there are 2m valid n bits words. However, with n bits, there are
2n different combinations of words. The difference, 2n – 2m, indicates the
number of erroneous words (see Figure 7.6).
Figure 7.6. Valid words and incorrect words
To avoid errors, it is advantageous for the 2m codewords to be maximally
distinct from each other. Such an approach prevents healthy codewords
from being transformed into other healthy codewords due to errors, thereby
ensuring effective error detection.
Generally speaking, the Hamming distance d(m1,m2) between two
codewords m1 and m2 is defined as the number of different bits of the same
rank by which they differ. For example, if m1 = 10110010 and m2 =
10000110, then d(m1,m2) = 3. The greater the Hamming distance between
two codewords, the easier it is to distinguish them. Specifically, if d(m1,m2)
= a, it takes a properly placed errors to transform m1 into m2.
For each code, it is important to define the minimum Hamming distance
dmin that allows the codewords to be sufficiently separated. Suppose that p
errors occur in a codeword. These p errors will be detected if dmin > p, i.e.
dmin ≥ p + 1. This establishes the primary rule to follow in the quest for
error detection and correction codes.

Figure 7.7. Rule R1
Rule R1: to detect p errors, it is necessary that dmin ≥ p + 1 (see Figure 7.7).
Thus, to detect simple errors (only one erroneous bit), dmin must be dmin ≥ 2.
Let us examine error correction up to an order q. Each codeword mi has a
sphere of influence where any erroneous codeword 
 (with 
) is considered to be mi. These spheres of influence must not touch or
overlap, necessitating that the minimum distance between any two
codewords be at least 2q + 1.
Figure 7.8. Rule R2
Rule R2: to correct errors up to order q, it is necessary that dmin ≥ 2q + 1
(see Figure 7.8).
Thus, correcting single errors (only one uncorrected bit) requires a code
with dmin ≥ 3.
Consider now the case where errors up to order p need detection, and errors
up to order q (where p > q) require correction. An erroneous codeword 
,
resulting from the introduction of q errors in a codeword mi, must not be
mistaken for a codeword mj such that 
. Therefore, it is
deduced that dmin must be greater than or equal to p + q + 1 to prevent
confusion between codewords.

Figure 7.9. Rule R3
Rule R3: to correct errors up to order q and detect errors up to order p
(where p > q), it is necessary that dmin ≥ p + q + 1 (see Figure 7.9).
7.2. Linear codes
A linear code is a systematic (n, m) block code in which the r = n − m
control bits depend linearly on the m bits of useful information:

EXAMPLE 7.1.–
Consider a code (6,3) whose matrices G and H are:

000000 001110 010101 011011 100011 101101 110110 111000
It is evident that dmin = 3, thus allowing the correction of all single

EXAMPLE 7.2.–
Let the code be (8,7) with H = [1 1 1 1 1 1 1 1]
The relation H.Y = 0 leads, with the modulo 2 operation, to a1 = x1 + x2
+ x3 + x4 + x5 + x6 + x7.
Figure 7.12. Parity bit
In this example, the bit a1 is known as the parity bit, and the codewords
have the characteristic of having an even number of 1. This allows 8-bit
characters to be represented using 7 bits for the ASCII code, with the eighth
bit being the parity bit. Note that the parity bit can be placed wherever
desired (see Figure 7.12).
With this system, if two characters in the ASCII code differ by only one bit,
they will also differ in the parity bit. Therefore, the minimum distance dmin
is equal to 2, which only allows the detection of simple errors.

Figure 7.13. VRC and LRC
However, it is possible to improve protection against errors by adding a
“longitudinal” parity check in addition to the “vertical” parity check
described previously (LRC: Longitudinal Redundancy Check; VRC:
Vertical Redundancy Check). Such a method consists of adding a control
character after every group of b blocks (see Figure 7.13).
Figure 7.14 shows the serial transmission of these blocks.
Figure 7.14. Serial transmission
With the addition of the VRC + LRC parity check method, groups of blocks
differing by just 1 useful information bit will also differ in VRC bit, LRC
bit and VRC + LRC bit. Consequently, the minimum distance dmin = 4
allows for the detection of single and double errors, as well as the
correction of single errors.
7.3. Polynomial codes
Polynomial codes are systematic linear codes commonly used in data
transmission for error detection, widely used in modern applications.
Consider an m-bit message X written as follows:

where the bit numbering used here is slightly different from before but
traditional in polynomial code applications. A polynomial X(z) is associated
with the message X:
For polynomial codes, used to represent binary data, allow these
polynomials to be added (modulo 2) and multiplied according to standard
algebraic rules. In a polynomial code, all codewords are represented by
polynomials Y(z) that are multiples of a generating polynomial H(z). This
type of code is known as linear and systematic because each polynomial
Y(z) can be expressed in the form:
where Q(z) is a polynomial whose degree is less than that of H(z) and
represents the message to be transmitted. The generator polynomial H(z) is
used for detecting and correcting transmission errors.
To transition from the useful information represented by the polynomial
X(z) to the coded information represented by the polynomial Y(z), a
polynomial code (n, m) is defined where r = n − m control bits are added.

Polynomial codes are widely used in telecomputing, with several well-
known examples:
CCITT V.41 code: the generating polynomial is H(z) = z16 + z12 + z5 +
1, used in the HDLC procedure.

CRC code 16: the generating polynomial is H(z) = z16 + z15 + z2 + 1,
used in the BSC procedure with EBCDIC coding.
CRC code 12: the generating polynomial is H(z) = z12 + z11 + z3 + z2 +
z + 1, used in the BSC procedure with 6-bit character coding.
ARPA code: the generating polynomial is H(z) = z24 + z23 + z17 + z16 +
z15 + z13 + z12 + z11 + z10 + z9 + z8 + z5 + z3 + 1.
Ethernet code: the generating polynomial is H(z) = z32 + z26 + z23 + z22
+ z16 + z12 + z11 + z10 + z8 + z7 + z5 + z4 + z2 + z + 1.
A significant subset of polynomial codes consists of cyclic codes. A
polynomial code (n, m) is cyclic if its generator polynomial H(z) divides zn
+ 1:
where Ω(z) is a polynomial of degree (n − m).
Cyclic codes possess a fundamental property that makes them particularly
advantageous: any circular shift (i.e. any circular permutation of bits) of a
codeword remains a valid codeword. This property greatly simplifies their
practical implementation and enhances their utility across various
telecommunications applications. Cyclic codes find widespread application
in wireless communication protocols such as Wi-Fi and Bluetooth.
7.4. Convolutional codes
A convolutional code, also known as a recurrent (n, m) code, encodes the
block based on the m bits of information in the block and the m − 1
previous bits or blocks.
Let us consider the example of the convolutional code (3,1) where 1 bit of
useful information corresponds to 3 bits of encoded data. The encoder is
shown in Figure 7.16, where the addition is performed using the “exclusive
or” operation, denoted by ⊕ (i.e. for which 1 ⊕ 1 = 0).

Figure 7.16. Convolutional code (3,1)
Thus, each transmitted bit xi gives rise to a triplet y1i, y2i, y3i. Errors can be
detected by calculating the values of xi, xi−1 and xi−2 (the latter two already
received) as functions of y1i, y2i, y3i, allowing for error checking.
The operation of convolutional coding can be represented in several ways:
a. Representation by tree diagram (see Figure 7.17). This representation
visualizes the various coding stages from useful information to the
codeword. It is well suited for convolutional codes, where nodes
represent encoder states and branches correspond to different possible
transitions based on input and output bits.

Figure 7.17. Tree diagram
b. Representation by state diagram (see Figure 7.18). This also visualizes
encoder states and explicitly shows transitions between states based on
input and output bits. Such a representation is particularly suitable for
convolutional codes.
Figure 7.18. State diagram
In Figure 7.18, the nodes correspond to the states of registers R1 and R2 (the
bits xi−1 and xi−2). The arcs correspond to the output states y1y2y3 depending
on whether a 0 or a 1 is input.
c. Representation by lattice diagram (see Figure 7.19). This diagram is
especially suitable for block type codes. It provides a visual

representation of all the processing steps performed on each block and
the various error correction procedures. Nodes within the lattice
represent distinct processing states, branches indicate potential
transitions based on input and output bits, and paths in the lattice
illustrate the specific processing steps applied to the blocks.
Figure 7.19. Lattice diagram

8
Encryption Systems
CONCEPTS COVERED IN THIS CHAPTER.–
Information protection is a concern for all users of binary information.
With the rise of website hacking, maintaining information
confidentiality has become crucial. This chapter explains the main
methods of data encryption.
Following a review of the standard methods of substitution and
transposition, two contemporary systems will be detailed: the DES
(Data Encryption Standard) system with a secret key and the RSA
(Rivest–Shamir–Adleman) system with a public key, highlighting the
use of prime numbers in the latter.
The method of elliptic curves will be introduced, building on Chapter 6
on arithmetic.
Finally, the potential of the quantum computer will be discussed, along
with an overview of encryption methods based on quantum mechanics.
References: [BEL 94, GHE 08, GOM 13, INA 99, LEH 19, LOV 91,
SIN 99].
8.1. Substitution and transposition
Encryption consists of transforming plain text into indecipherable text
(cipher text) using an algorithm and an encryption key. The opposite
operation is decryption, which recovers the plain text from the cipher text
using the corresponding decryption key. Generally speaking, two keys are
needed in a symmetric encryption system, one for encryption (encryption
key) and one for decryption (decryption key), as shown in Figure 8.1.

Figure 8.1. Schema of an encryption system
If the key K1 is equal to the key K2 (i.e. K1 = K2), the encryption system is
called classic or single key. This means that a single key is used for both
encryption and decryption of data.
On the contrary, if the key K1 is different from the key K2 (i.e. K1 ≠ K2),
the encryption system is called a key pair. In this case, one key (K1) is used
for data encryption and another separate key (K2) is used for decryption.
EXAMPLE 8.1.–
Construction of a key: choice of a particular word (see Figure 8.2.)
Figure 8.2. Encryption table
As shown in Figure 8.2, the cipher alphabet starts with the key
“juliscaer”, followed by the remaining unused letters in alphabetical
order starting from “r”. Using this cipher alphabet, the French sentence
“le ciel est bleu”1 can be encrypted.
There are two main basic techniques in the field of encryption: the
substitution method and the transposition method.

The substitution method: involves replacing each character or group of
characters in plain text with another character or group of characters
according to a predefined rule. For example, in monoalphabetic
substitution encryption, each letter of the plain text is replaced by a
corresponding letter of the alphabet according to a fixed
correspondence. This method can be done symmetrically with a single
key for encryption and decryption, or asymmetrically with two
separate keys.
The transposition method: involves reorganizing the order of the
characters in the plain text without modifying them. For example, in
transposition encryption, letters in the plain text are rearranged
according to a specific rule, sometimes using a matrix or key to
determine the order of permutation. This method does not change the
characters themselves but makes the cipher text more difficult to
understand without knowledge of the transposition method used.
8.2. Substitution methods
8.2.1. Replacement of a symbol by a symbol
Encrypting a text involves replacing each symbol (or character in the plain
text) with another symbol (or character) according to a specific rule.
Caesar’s code, also known as shift encryption, is a historical encryption
method attributed to Julius Caesar. According to the tradition reported by
Suetonius, such a method involves shifting each letter of the plain text three
positions to the right in the alphabet.
Figure 8.3. Caesar’s code
In a “modern” version of shift encryption, the ASCII code of a character is
adjusted by a fixed “offset”, also referred to as a key. The resulting value is
then reduced modulo the size of the alphabet.

EXAMPLE 8.2.–
Adding 3 to the ASCII code (see Figure 8.4) to encrypt the French word
“ordinateur”2
Figure 8.4. ASCII code offset
An important cryptographic tool is frequency analysis. By analyzing a large
number of texts, the frequency of appearance of different letters is
determined. For French texts, the results are shown in Figure 8.5.

Figure 8.5. Frequency analysis for French texts

statistically closest to R, consider replacing R with L (if this does not
work, other options can be explored!).

multiple times, indicating a promising direction.

substitution, we could consider Y → N, but no fitting word comes to

appropriate. However, Y → S would yield LAOSSE, suggesting the

VLUMAME must be PLUMAGE13, from which V → P. Therefore,

Another interesting encryption method worth noting is based on modulo
calculation, known as the Hill cipher. This technique uses a key represented
by a 2 × 2 matrix and segments the message into blocks of two letters. If the
total number of letters is odd, the last block is completed with the “@”
symbol.
To illustrate this method, here is an example.
