To determine the median graphically, we can use one of the following
methods:
Increase cumulative polygon: plot the increasing cumulative frequency
polygon. The median corresponds to the point where the cumulative
frequency reaches half 
 of the total frequency.

Decrease cumulative polygon: plot the decreasing cumulative
frequency polygon. The median corresponds to the point where the
cumulative frequency reaches half 
 of the total frequency.
Intersection of both polygons: plot both the increasing and decreasing
cumulative frequency polygons on the same graph. The abscissa (x-
coordinate) at the intersection of these two polygons provides the
value of the median.

EXAMPLE 10.24.–
In this example, 150 workers are classified according to their age, as
shown in Figure 10.30.
Figure 10.30. Statistical table
Draw the cumulative polygons as shown in Figure 10.31. The
intersection of the two polygons provides the median value xm = 35.3.
Figure 10.31. Cumulative polygons.
The notion of median can be generalized by introducing quartiles. Quartiles
divide the entire population into four equal parts, each representing 25% of
the dataset.
First quartile (Q1): represents the value that separates the lowest 25%
of the data from the rest. It corresponds to the median of the lower half

of the data.
Second quartile (Q2): also known as the median, Q2 divides the data
into two equal parts, with 50% of the data below and 50% above.
Third quartile (Q3): separates the highest 25% of the data from the
rest. It is equivalent to the median of the upper half of the data.
In Figure 10.32, these quartiles are graphically representation. The values of
Q1, Q2 (median) and Q3 are marked on the x-axis. The range between Q1
and Q3 is known as the interquartile range, which contains 50% of the data.
Figure 10.32. Quartiles
The total dataset can also be divided into 10 equal parts, known as deciles,
or into 100 equal parts, known as centiles:
Deciles: divide the data into 10 equal parts, with each part representing
10% of the total data. The corresponding values are called deciles.
Centiles: divide the data into 100 equal parts, with each part
representing 1% of the total data. The corresponding values are called
centiles.

10.4. Dispersion parameters
Dispersion parameters measure the spread of a statistical distribution. The
main types include the range, the interquartile range, the mean deviation
and the standard deviation.
10.4.1. Range, interquartile range, mean range
The extent of a statistical series is defined as the difference between the
extreme values of the character. The difference between the first quartile
(Q1) and third quartile (Q3) is known as the interquartile range. These
definitions are shown in Figure 10.37.

Figure 10.37. Extent and interquartile range.
Figure 10.38. Interdecile range.
The interdecile range and the intercentile range are defined analogously.
The interdecile range is the difference between the 9th and 1st deciles,
while the intercentile range is the difference between specified centiles.
These concepts are shown in Figure 10.38.
The arithmetic mean of the absolute deviations from the arithmetic mean is
called the mean deviation. It is calculated using the following formula:
where ni represents the frequency of the value xi and m is the arithmetic
mean.

10.4.3. Coefficient of variation and concentration index
When the scale of a character is expanded or contracted, the dispersion of a
statistical series is affected. Specifically, if xi, ρ, and m are transformed to
x′i, ρ′ and m′ with:
Although the standard deviation and the mean are altered by this
transformation, the ratio of these two quantities remains unchanged. This
ratio is known as the coefficient of variation:
The coefficient of variation is invariant under homothetic changes of scale,
allowing for comparisons between two statistical series with different
scales.
Given a statistical series (xi, ni), plotting the cumulative percentage of nixi
against the cumulative percentage of ni yields a curve known as the
concentration curve or Lorenz curve. This curve is typically represented
within a square with sides of length 100.

EXAMPLE 10.29.–
Modify the table in Figure 10.40 to include the percentages as shown in
Figure 10.41.
Figure 10.41. Percentage table
Draw the curve of the cumulative nixi percentages as a function of the
cumulative ni percentages (see Figure 10.42.).
Figure 10.42. Lorenz curve
The equipartition line divides the square into two regions of equal areas.

When the Lorenz curve deviates from this line, it indicates a higher
concentration of data. To measure this concentration, the concentration
index or Gini index is used. This index is defined by the area A between the
equipartition line and the Lorenz curve, relative to the area of the half-
square, which is 5,000. The Gini index is calculated as follows:
The Gini index ranges from 0 to 1. A value of 0 indicates perfect equality (0
concentration), meaning that the data is evenly distributed. Conversely, a
value of 1 indicates maximum concentration, where the entire area A (A =
5,000) is between the equipartition line and the Lorenz curve.
10.5. Linear adjustment
10.5.1. Principle of adjustment
A statistical series consists of a set of numerical data. To explore
relationships between these data, it is often useful to model them in a more
concise and understandable way. One common approach is fitting, which
involves finding a function ni = f(xi) that best represents the data. In
practice, the points (xi, ni) form a scatter of point, and the goal is to find a
curve that best fits this scatter.
In the remainder of the discussion, since the focus is on curves and their
graphical representation, sizes will be denoted as yi instead of ni, to obtain
the desired curve yi = f(xi).
The main methods for adjusting point scatter are:
1. Graphical adjustment improved by the mean point method: this
method involves drawing a curve that approximately passes through
the mean points of the grouping intervals, offering a simplified
graphical representation of the point scatter.
2. Mechanical adjustment by the methods of scaled averages or moving
averages: these methods calculate successive averages over subsets of

neighboring points, helping to obtain an adjustment curve by
smoothing out the fluctuations in the scatter points.
3. Analytical adjustment: this method uses the least squares technique to
determine the mathematical equation that best represents the
relationship between the variables in point scatter. It minimizes the
sum of the squares of the differences between the observed values and
those predicted by the adjustment curve.
For a graphical adjustment, the principle is relatively simple: it involves
drawing a freehand curve that appears to best fit among the scatter points
(see Figure 10.43). This method has a significant drawback: the adjustment
obtained is imprecise and dependent on the designer’s judgment. However,
it also offers two important advantages: first, it helps in identifying and
potentially eliminating “outlier” points that may be excluded in other
adjustment methods; second, it is characterized by its simplicity and low
cost, as it requires only basic drawing skills.
Figure 10.43. Graphic adjustment.
To obtain a more precise fitting curve, the average point method can be
used. This method involves connecting successive points so as to form
triangles and then drawing a median for each triangle (note that the median,
in the geometric sense, is the line segment connecting a vertex of a triangle
to the midpoint of the opposite side). Next, the midpoints of these medians
are connected (see Figure 10.44). This approach results in a smoother curve
that better captures the overall trends in the point scatter.

Figure 10.44. Mean point method.
The principle of mechanical adjustment involves replacing the series of
pairs (xi, yi) with a new series of pairs 
, where  are calculated from
yi using a specific method. Two main processes are commonly used:
The scaled average method
The pairs are divided into groups, as shown in Figure 10.45.
Figure 10.45. Scaled average method

line segments. Another variation involves applying a weighting to the
central element. For example, for the point

Graphical and mechanical adjustments provide an approximation of the
adjustment curve’s shape. Next, a curve of known type, represented by the
equation y = f(x), is chosen. This equation includes several parameters for
which optimal values need to be determined.
For example, for a line with the equation y = ax + b, the parameters to be
determined are a and b. For a parabola with the equation y = ax2 + bx + c,
the parameters to be determined are a, b and c. Determining these
parameters is the goal of analytical fitting, with the least square’s method
being the most commonly used technique.

Consider Figure 10.47, which shows a few points and the curve y = f(x)
supposed to best represent these points.

curve (xi, y′i), where y′i = f(xi), is evaluated. The difference in absolute value
between the ordinates of these two points is then measured:

requires solving to find the optimal parameters.

10.6. Chronological series
10.6.1. Introduction
A chronological series, or temporal series, is a statistical series that
represents changes in particular magnitude over time. Chronological series
are commonly used to analyze economic, social or scientific phenomena.
In this section, a chronological series is used to illustrate variations in the
number of job applications in France from 1973 to 1978, expressed in
thousands. These data sourced from an article published in Le Monde on
April 24, 1979 (see Figure 10.51).

Figure 10.51. Change in the number of job applications
The variation of a socio-economic phenomenon over time generally results
from four fundamental components:
The long-term movement or general trend: this represents the overall
direction and evolution of the phenomenon over an extended period. It
can be ascending (upward), descending (downward) or stable.
Seasonal movement: this includes regular and recurring variations
observed at specific times of the year. These variations are typically
linked to seasonal factors such as holidays, agricultural cycles and
festivals.
Accidental variations: these are due to unforeseen and non-periodic
events such as strikes, droughts, floods and wars. These variations are
often regarded as temporary disturbances in the time series.
Cyclical movement: this results from the periodicity of economic
phases such as and recession. The importance and impact of cyclical
movements can be subject to debate among economists.
The study of a chronological series involves separating and identifying its
different components. In this section, the focus will be on analyzing the
trend and seasonal movement (at the monthly level in the chosen example).

Two models can be used to decompose a time series:
1. The additive model: in this model, the different components are treated
as distinct elements that add together. The notations used are as
follows: yi represents the raw data, 
 represents the data
corresponding to the general trend, si denotes the seasonal component
and ei accounts for accidental variations and cyclical movement.
According to this model, the relationship is expressed as:
In other words, the observed value yi is the sum of the general trend 
,
the seasonal component si and the accidental variations ei.
2. The multiplicative model: in this model, the components are treated as
multiplicative factors that interact with each other. In this case, it is
assumed that the seasonal component is proportional to the general
trend. The relationship is given by:
where ci = (1 + αi) is called the seasonal coefficient.
Depending on the nature of the data, we might choose between the additive
and multiplicative models. However, the multiplicative model is generally
preferred for its ability to account for proportional variations.

Figure 10.52. Change year by year.
In the study, it is possible to identify distinct periods within which seasonal
variations can be observed. In this example, periods correspond to years
and seasons to months. Figures 10.52 and 10.53 show the repeatability of
seasons (months) within each period (year), helping to visualize seasonal
variations and their regularity over time.
For the analysis, the term ei, will be disregarded, simplifying the seasonal
coefficient to:

Figure 10.53. Change month by month.
Assuming that the seasonal component is independent of the period and
only depends on the season, it is necessary to calculate 12 seasonal
coefficients, each corresponding to a specific month.
Additionally, it is assumed that seasonal increases are offset by seasonal
decreases, meaning that seasonal fluctuations balance out.
For an additive model, this results in the condition that the sum of the
seasonal coefficients for all seasons of a given period is equal to zero: Σj sj
= 0, where the index “j” represents the seasons of a given period.
For a multiplicative model, the sum of the seasonal coefficients must equal
the total number of seasons, i.e. Σj cj = (number of seasons) (in this
example, Σj cj = 12). This hypothesis may not always be valid for every
time series.
10.6.2. Study of the general trend
The general trend in a time can be obtained using one of the following
methods:

the scaled average method or the moving average method;
analytical adjustment.
Each method has its advantages, and the choice depends on the nature of
the data and the specific requirements of the analysis.
In the example, the scaled average method is first used to determine the
general trend by calculating the averages for each year (see Figure 10.54).
This method involves averaging over successive periods to smooth out

Figure 10.57. Determination of seasonal coefficients
The monthly seasonal coefficients obtained can be applied in two different
ways:
1. Seasonal adjustment:
Objective: to remove the seasonal component from the raw data.
Method: divide the raw data by the corresponding monthly
seasonal coefficients. This process yields seasonally adjusted
data, which highlights the underlying trend and helps to analyze
the general trend of the phenomenon without seasonal effects.
Formula:

2. Seasonal reconstruction:
Objective: to model or reconstruct the original data based on the
identified general trend and seasonal effects.
Method: multiply the trend values by the seasonal coefficient for
each period. This method combines the general trend with the
seasonal component to approximate the original data. Although
simple, this method provides a basic representation of seasonal
variations.
Formula :
b. Method of reporting to the general trend

10.7. Covariance and correlation
10.7.1. Regression lines
To perform a linear fit on two-dimensional statistical series, consider the set
of triples (xi, yj, nij), where xi is a value of the first character, yj is a value of
the second character and nij represents the frequency or count associated
with the pair (xi,yj). Such a series is typically displayed in a crosstab format
(see example 10.9) and can be visualized using a scatter plot, as shown in
Figure 10.24.
To fit a line to this scatter plot, we can seek a linear relationship of the form
y = f(x), or x = g(y).
In this section, the focus is on linear adjustments using straight lines. For a
linear fit, the goal is to find a line of the form y = ax + b, where a and b are
the parameters to be determined.
For the curve y = f(x) = ax + b, which represents the straight line Dx/y:

10.7.2. Linear correlation
The difference between the regression lines indicates the strength of the
correlation, in this case, linear. If the lines nearly overlap, it suggests a high
correlation. Conversely, if the lines are nearly perpendicular, it implies a
weak or even zero correlation. To obtain a more precise measure of
correlation, calculate the product a × a′:
This quantity is always non-negative, leading to Schwarz’s inequality:

The correlation is maximum when the regression lines are perfectly aligned,
which implies:
In this case, Schwarz’s equality holds:
Correlation is zero when the lines are perpendicular, which implies
Since a × a′ ≥ 0, the only possible value for a × a′ in this case is zero.
However, instead of using a × a′ directly, the square root of this product is
used as the measure of correlation, known as the linear correlation
coefficient, denoted by r:
The range of variation of the correlation coefficient r is between -1 and +1.
Extreme values of r correspond to a strong correlation, while a value of 0
indicates no correlation (see Figure 10.66).
Figure 10.66. Overview of r values
The linear correlation coefficient r can also be expressed using the
covariance of x and y as well as their standard deviations σ(x) and σ(y).

The linear correlation coefficient has two key properties: invariance by
translation and invariance by change of scale.

Important warning: although correlation can suggest a mutual influence
between two traits or even imply a cause-and-effect relationship, it is
essential to consider the limitations of correlation analysis:
a. Determining causality: it is generally challenging to ascertain with
certainty which of the two variables, x or y, is the cause and which is
the effect, unless it is clearly evident from the context or experimental
design.
b. Spurious correlations: it is crucial to be aware of “accidental” or even
far-fetched correlations. A strong correlation does not necessarily
imply a real causal relationship. For an entertaining look at some
surprising correlations, the reader may visit the website:
https://tylervigen.com/spurious-correlations.
c. Third-variable influence: it is also important to recognize that x and y
might be related to a third variable. For instance, while a strong
correlation might exist between sunglasses sales and ice cream sales,
the underlying factor could be temperature. Higher temperatures may
lead to increased sales of both sunglasses and ice cream, without a
direct causal link between the two variables.

11
Probability Laws and Simulation
CONCEPTS COVERED IN THIS CHAPTER.–
This chapter serves as a bridge between the previous chapters on
probability (Chapter 9) and statistics (Chapter 10).
After covering the fundamental concepts of random variables – both
discrete and continuous – various types of event occurrence modeling,
known as probability distributions (laws), are explored.
These descriptions are described in terms of probability expressions,
mathematical expectation and standard deviation, with a focus on
common distributions such as the uniform, binomial, Poisson,
exponential and normal distributions.
The field of computer science has facilitated the development of
simulation techniques that recreate real phenomena on a computer.
These simulations rely on random sampling according to specific
probability distributions, which are modeled using generating programs.
This chapter concludes with the application of simulation to a classic
problem in queuing theory, providing a detailed examination of the
M/M/1 queue model.
References: [PHE 77, COC 19].
11.1. Random variables
Suppose that an experiment can produce several results under identical
conditions, assuming for the moment that their number is finite and denoted
by A, B, …, K. The appearance of these results is random, and it is
convenient to associate the value of a random variable X with each result.
For example:

X = 1 for event A,
X = 2 for event B,
X = 3 for event C,
etc.
The probabilities of occurrence of events are then denoted as follows:
p(event A) = p(X = 1),
p(event B) = p(X = 2),
etc.
The probability p(X) can be considered a function of the variable X. This
function, p(X), is known as a probability law and characterizes the
distribution of possible results of the random experiment.

EXAMPLE 11.1.–
A jar contains 2 white balls and 4 black balls. Apart from their color, the
balls are identical. Two balls are drawn at random. The possible events
are as follows:
A. 2 white balls.
B. 1 white ball and 1 black ball.
C. 2 black balls.
The random variable X is defined such that:
X: 1 for event A;
X: 2 for event B;
X: 3 for event C.
To determine the probabilities of these events in terms of X, note that
the jar contains a total of 6 balls.
For event A (2 white balls), there is only one way to draw 2 white balls,
which is by drawing the two white balls available in the jar.
To find the probability of event A (i.e. X = 1):

needs to be determined.

There are 2 white balls and 4 black balls. The number of ways to choose
1 white ball from 2 and 1 black ball from 4 is calculated as follows:
Number of ways to choose a white ball and a black ball = (number of
ways to choose 1 white ball from 2) × (number of ways to choose 1
black ball from 4)

The probability law p(X) is shown in Figure 11.1. This is a discrete
probability law because the random variable X takes on discrete values.

Figure 11.1. Graphical representation of the probability law.
In the previous example, the values taken by the random variable X serve
primarily to “count” the events, such as indicating whether 2 white balls, 1
white ball and 1 black ball or 2 black balls are drawn. In this case, X simply
labels the outcomes of the random experiment. However, a random variable
can also take on values with specific, meaningful interpretations. For
instance, consider example 11.2, where the values of the random variable
are not just labels but represent quantities with inherent significance.

EXAMPLE 11.2.–
A dice game works as follows: the die is thrown four times. The
player’s gain or loss is determined by the number of times a “1”
appears.
The player wins 2 euros each time a “1” appears and loses 2 euros
otherwise. For 4 rolls of the die, there are 5 possible situations, each
representing the player’s gain or loss. These outcomes are used to
define the value of a random variable X, which reflects the player’s net
gain or loss.
Case 1: “1” never comes out. The player loses 2 euros four times,
leading a total loss of 8 euros. Thus, the value of the random
variable is therefore X = −8.
Case 2: “1” appears exactly once. The player wins 2 euros once but
loses 6 euros, resulting in a net loss of 4 euros. Therefore, X = −4.
Case 3:“1” appears exactly twice. The player wins 2 euros and
loses 2 euros, resulting in no net gain or loss. Thus, X = 0.
Case 4: “1” appears exactly three times. The player wins 6 euros
and loses 2 euros, resulting in a net gain of 4 euros. Hence, X = 4.
Case 5: “1” appears four times. The player wins 8 euros, resulting
in a net gain of 8 euros. Thus, X = 8.
The random variable X thus takes 5 discrete values: −8; −4, 0, +4 and +
8. When rolling a six-sided die, the probability of rolling a “1” is
effectively , while the probability of rolling a number other than “1” is
. To simplify the notations, let p represent the probability of rolling a
“1” (i.e. 
) and q represent the probability of rolling any other
number (i.e. 
).
Calculate the probabilities corresponding to the different situations as
follows:
Probability that no “1” appears (therefore multiplying the probability q
four times by itself) comes out during the four throws of the die:

Probability of rolling a “1” exactly once during the four rolls of the die:
p(X = −2) = (Number of ways to roll a “1” exactly once in 4 throws) ×
(Product of the probabilities that “1” comes out 1 time and that it does
not come out three times).
Probability of rolling a “1” exactly twice during the four rolls of the die:
p(X = 0) = (Number of ways to roll a “1” exactly two times out of 4
throws) × (Probability of rolling a “1” twice) × (Probability of not
rolling a “1” twice).
Probability for a 1 to roll exactly three times during the four rolls of the
die:
p(X = 4) = (number of times to see 1 come out three times out of 4
throws) × (probability that 1 comes out three times) × (probability that 1
does not come out).
Probability of rolling a “1” exactly four times during the four rolls of the
die:

p(X = 8) = (Number of ways to roll a “1” exactly four times out of 4
throws) × (Probability of rolling a “1” in each throw).
The corresponding probability law is graphically represented in Figure
14.2.
Figure 11.2. Graphical representation of the probability law.
Note that, in general, the probability is given by the following

choose k objects from n total objects. This formula is applicable within
the framework of the binomial probability distribution, also known as
Bernoulli’s law. This formula will be used for calculations later on.
A random variable can take real values, which can be either discrete or
continuous. In the previous examples, the values were discrete, indicating
that the probability law is discrete.

For continuous random variables, the probability is determined using the
concept of probability density. Consider the example of selecting a random
point on a line segment AB. To locate the points of the segment, the line
containing the segment is oriented as an axis, with an origin defining the
abscissa x. The position of any point on the segment is given by its abscissa
x.
The abscissa of the endpoints A and B of the segment are a and b
(where a < b).
A point M of the segment has an abscissa x such that a ≤ x ≤ b.
Choosing a point at random is the equivalent to selecting x randomly
between a and b.
Consider X the random variable with abscissa x as its values (see Figure
11.3). To characterize the distribution of possible values of X, the concept of
probability density is introduced.
Figure 11.3. Problem situation
A segment contains an infinite number of points, so the variable X has an
infinite range of values. Choosing an abscissa point x represents a choice
among an infinite set, which results in a probability of zero. Therefore, the
approach must be modified to not consider the probability of finding a point
“exactly at x”, but rather the probability of finding a point within the
interval dx as it will be greater for larger dx and smaller for smaller dx.
Hence, there should be a proportional relationship between dx and the
probability, denoted as dp (by analogy with dx), i.e.
This notation expresses the relationship between the interval dx and the
probability dp. The “proportionality coefficient” f(x) can generally depend
on the value x. The term f(x) represents the probability density

In the case of a continuous random variable where each point is equally
likely, f(x) does not depend on x and is therefore constant.
The probability density function must satisfy the following conditions:
It must be non-negative: f(x) ≥ 0.
It must be continuous over the interval [a, b].
The integral of the probability density function over the interval [a, b]
must be equal to:
This condition has a graphical interpretation: the area under the curve y =
f(x), between the x-axis and the vertical lines x = a and x = b, must be equal
to 1 (see Figure 11.4).
Figure 11.4. Probability density.
If a and b are finite, then f(x) = 0 for x < a and x > b. In the example where
f(x) = k is a constant, the integral

leads to:
This result is shown in Figure 11.5.

Figure 11.5. Probability density.

11.2. Mathematical expectation, variance and
standard deviation
Definitions similar to those seen in statistics are used, but in probability
theory, the term “mathematical expectation” is employed instead of
“average”. The mathematical expectation is an average weighted by the
probability values.
Given a random variable X associated with a probability law p(X), the
average or mathematical expectation E(X) is defined differently depending
on whether the variable is discrete or continuous:
Mathematical expectation has linearity properties: for two random variables

11.3. Distribution function
The distribution function of a probability law of a random variable X is
defined as:

The cumulative distribution function provides the probabilities that the
random variable X takes on a value less than or equal to x.
F(x) ranges between 0 and 1, as it represents the cumulative
probability from the smallest value of X (which could be −∞) up to x.
Since the total probability is 1 and all probabilities are non-negative,
F(x) is always an increasing function.
For a discrete random variable, F(x) is a “step” function, meaning it
has jumps at the points where the random variable X takes specific
values.
For a continuous random variable, F(x) is a continuous function,
reflecting the continuous nature of the underlying probability density.

For the discrete variable X in example 11.1, which takes the values 1, 2
and 3 with probabilities 
,

, respectively.

11.4. Usual probability laws
Some probability laws frequently encountered in experiments include the
uniform probability law, the binomial (or Bernoulli) law, Poisson’s law, the
exponential law and the normal (or Gaussian) law.
11.4.1. Uniform law
The uniform law is one of the simplest probability laws and is used to
model continuous random variables with probability density f(x) that is
constant over a given interval (a, b) and zero outside this interval. This law

ensures that each value within the interval has an equal probability of being
observed (see Figure 11.5). The density function is denoted as follows: f(x)
= C (where C is a constant) with the condition on the given interval (a, b):
This implies that the constant C can be calculated as follows:

11.4.2. Binomial or Bernoulli’s law
The binomial law is frequently used to model repeated experiments (trials)
of the same type with binary results. It is based on a Bernoulli scheme and
is associated with discrete random variables. In this law, it is assumed that
the trials are identical and independent of each other. This law was
illustrated in example 11.2., where a specific case of the binomial law was
studied, and probabilities of success in a certain number of repeated trials
were calculated.
In each trial of a binomial experiment, there are two possible results: the
event of interest, with an elementary probability p, and its complement,
with a probability q = 1 − p. When n trials are conducted, the number of
times the event of interest occurs is denoted by k. This value k is assigned to
a random variable X, which can take discrete values from 0 to n. Thus, in
the context of the binomial law, X represents the number of successes

(occurrences of the event of interest) in the n trials. It is a discrete random
variable that can take values between 0 and n, inclusive.
The probability law associated with the Bernoulli diagram is expressed by:
where 
 is the binomial coefficient defined by:
Recall Newton’s binomial formula on this subject.
Figure 11.9. Binomial law.
This formula guarantees us that the sum of all the probabilities of the
binomial law is equal to 1. Since q = 1 − p, it follows that (p + q)n = 1, and
therefore,

Figure 11.9 provides the graphical representation of this law for 
 and n
= 10.
Note that this law depends only on two parameters: n and p. Therefore, it is
sometimes denoted as B (n, p).

To calculate the variance v(X), an alternative approach can be used.
Consider Xi as the random variable for an individual test. Here, Xi = 1 if the
expected event occurs and Xi = 0 if the opposite event occurs. Thus, Xi
follows Bernoulli’s law with a success probability p. The expected value is
E(Xi) = 1 × p + 0 × q = p, and the variance is v(Xi) = p × (1 − p)2 + q × (0 −
p)2 = pq(p + q) = pq.
The variable X of the Bernouilli scheme (n identical and independent trials)
can be considered as the sum of the variables X1, X2, …, Xn, each
corresponding to a single trial. The variables Xi are independent. Therefore:

The exponential law is characteristic of memoryless systems, sometimes
referred to as the law of amnesia. Consider two instants on the time axis: t
and t + h (h ≥ 0). A random variable X is memoryless if it satisfies the
Markov property:

[P]. Furthermore, it can be demonstrated (accepted as true) that a
memoryless random variable follows the exponential law.
To illustrate this concretely, consider a scenario where individuals arrive in
a queue at an average rate of λ = 4 individuals per minute, with arrivals
following Poisson’s law. In this case, the time θ between two consecutive
arrivals has an average of 
 minute. This time θ follows an exponential
law with parameter λ = 4, corresponding to an average of  minute.
Poisson’s law and the exponential law are complementary and play
significant roles in queue simulation. Poisson’s law models the number of
arrivals within a given time frame, while the exponential law describes the
time between consecutive arrivals. Together, these laws provide a
comprehensive framework for analyzing and simulating queue behavior.
Furthermore, a relationship exists between the exponential law and
Poisson’s law. The following table illustrates this relationship:

To conclude on normal distribution, it is crucial to highlight its universal
character, as demonstrated by the central limit theorem. This theorem
asserts that, regardless of the original distribution of a population, the
distribution of the sample mean approaches a normal distribution as the
sample size becomes large enough. This property reveals the widespread
nature of the normal distribution across various fields and scenarios,
reinforcing its significance in statistical analysis and real-world
applications.

It is important to note that the central limit theorem makes no assumptions
about the specific probability law followed by each random variable Xi. The
theorem applies regardless of the underlying distribution, provided that the
variables are independent and identically distributed with a finite mean and
variance.
11.5. General information on simulation
Simulation is a popular technique for reproducing real-world events using a
computer (a modern approach, as earlier simulations were performed
manually). This method uses randomness, which might seem paradoxical at
first because randomness is typically viewed as inherently unpredictable.
The idea of incorporating chance into a simulation might suggest a mastery
over randomness, which is often thought to be impossible. And yet…
11.5.1. Weak law of large numbers
A fundamental mathematical result underlying simulation techniques is the
Law of Large Numbers. Intuitively, this law suggests that a statistical result
should approximate the notion of probability. It forms the basics of
statistics, which, by analyzing large samples (or, in mathematical terms,

large numbers), aims to estimate the probability of events. But what exactly
constitutes “large” in this context?
Two fundamental theorems justify this intuition: the Weak Law of Large
Numbers (originally developed by Jacques Bernoulli) and the Strong Law of
Large Numbers. To understand these laws, it is necessary to define what is
meant by the convergence of statistical frequency towards probability.
The Weak Law of Large Numbers, on the contrary, asserts that the
probability of the empirical average deviating from the theoretical average
becomes increasingly smaller as the sample size grows. In other words,
with a sufficiently larger sample, the empirical average will “almost surely”
approach the theoretical average.
The Strong Law of Large Numbers, on the contrary, asserts that the
probability of the empirical average deviates from the theoretical average
becomes increasingly smaller as the sample size grows. In other words,
with a sufficiently sample, the empirical average “almost surely”
approaches the theoretical average.
In the case of rolling a die, it is expected that if the number of times “1”
appears is averaged over a large number of rolls, the result should
approximate 
, which is the probability p of rolling a “1”. To formalize
this, introduce the random variable Xi, which takes the value “1” if “1”
appears on the i-th throw, and 0 otherwise.
Define the statistical frequency fn as the average of the results obtained
from n throws:
where xi is the result of the i-th throw.
The statistical frequency fn represents the proportion of times of “1” appears
among the n throws. According to the Law of Large Numbers, as the
number of throws n becomes large, this statistical frequency should
approach the theoretical probability 
. Thus, with an increasing number
of throws, fn should get closer and closer to the theoretical probability of the

rolling a “1”. This convergence is a key result of probability theory and
justifies the expectation that the frequency of occurrence of “1” converges
to  when the number of throws is sufficiently large.
To provide a practical mathematical result related to this convergence, we
consider the Bienaymé–Chebyshev inequality. Consider a random variable
X with mean μ and standard deviation σ, taking discrete values x1., x2, … .,
xn with corresponding probabilities p1, p2, … ., pn. Its variance is given by:
Now, focus on the event A defined by |X − μ| ≥ ε, where “ε” is a positive
real number. Let i(A) denote the indices i that correspond to this event. It
follows that:
As a result
Hence, the Bienaymé–Chebyshev inequality is:
Consider the following random variable
defined as the average of a series of independent random variables Xi with
the same probability law (mean μ and standard deviation σ). For this

variable, the mathematical expectation and variance are given by:
Applying the Bienaymé–Chebyshev inequality to

of large numbers.
WEAK LAW OF LARGE NUMBERS.–
Consider a sequence of independent and identically distributed random
variables, each with mean μ and standard deviation σ. Let 
 be defined
as the average of thee first n random variables in the sequence:
Then, for every ε > 0, as n tends to infinity (∞), the probability
 tends to 1.
This theorem implies that it is highly improbable for the difference 
to be greater than ε when n is large. In simpler terms, 
 converges to μ in
probability as n approaches infinity. It is important to note that this result,
initially established by Bernoulli, concerns the limit of probability.

For practical applications, consider an experiment in which a particular
event A is expected. For this experiment, associate a random variable Xi
(where the index i accounts for different trials of the experiment) that takes
the value 1 if event A occurs and the value 0 if event A does not occur. The
mathematical expectation of the variable Xi is defined as follows:
If the experiment is repeated n times and the statistical frequency fn, which
corresponds to the empirical mean 
, is calculated, the weak law of large
numbers indicates that as n becomes sufficiently large, it is very likely that
fn will be close to E(Xi), which is the theoretical probability p(A). In other
words, the statistical frequency and the theoretical probability should be
similar for very large n, reflecting the practical principle of the weak law of
large numbers.
However, it is important to note that the weak law of large numbers does
not claim that the statistical frequency fn converges to the probability p(A)
as n becomes very large. Instead, it states that as n becomes very large, the
probability that the absolute difference between the statistical frequency fn
and the theoretical probability p(A) is less than a given ∈ tends to 1. This
means that fn and p(A) will be close for a sufficiently large n.

In this section, several examples will be presented to illustrate how a
simulation operates in practice.

crosses the gaps between the strips is denoted by n. Buffon

Buffon conducted this experiment manually, as computers did not exist
in his time. Today, computers can be used to perform this simulation.
For instance, with the parameter L = 6, d = 3, and N = 1,000, a single
simulation might yield an approximation of π = 3.02543. To improve
accuracy, it is necessary to repeat the experiment many times and
average the results obtained.

EXAMPLE 11.13.–
Here is another example illustrating the use of chance. Suppose the goal
is to estimate the area of a surface bounded by a closed curve, such as a
disk centered at the origin with radius R. Theoretically, the area of the
disk is given by A = πR2. Now, consider that this disk is inscribed in a
square with side length 2R, as shown in Figure 11.16.
Figure 11.16. Areas for throwing points.
The area of the square is also known and is given by: = (2R)2 = 4R2.
To estimate the area of the disk, a computer can be used to randomly
and uniformly choose points (x, y) within the square. Specifically,
values for x and y will be generated such that 0 ≤ x ≤ 2 and 0 ≤ y ≤ 2.
Suppose N points are randomly thrown, and n of those points are
identified as being inside the disk. The points that lie inside the disk
must satisfy the condition x2 + y2 < R2. Given that the distribution of
points within both the square and the disk is uniform, the number of
points inside the disk is proportional to the area of the disk. Thus, 
, where A is the area of the disk and B is the area of the square.
Since B = 4R2, the area of the disk A can be calculated as:

This provides an “experimental” value of the area of a disk. Given that
the theoretical area of the disk is 4R2, the “experimental” value of π can
be approximated by:
For example, with N = 500 points, an experiment yields an approximate
value of π = 3.152. Repeating the experiment, a second time results in π
= 3.04, and a third time yields π = 3.216. This process is continued up to
10 times. Taking the average of the results gives π ≈ 3.114, which is a
reasonably close approximation to the “theoretical” value of π.

is a well-known curve, although its name might not be familiar: it is the

EXAMPLE 11.15.–
Consider the following game. A player starts with 1 euro. They roll a
die, and if the result is even, they win 1 euro; if the result is odd, they
lose 1 euro. The game continues until the player has either 0 euros or 5
euros. The goal is to determine the average duration of the game,
expressed in the number of dice throws. To estimate this, a simulation
program can be used, which can be represented by the following
algorithm:
Input N // number of parts
S = 0 // accumulation of durations j obtained in the games
For i = 1 to N
      j = 0
      M = 1 // initial amount
      While (M > 0 and M < 5) // while M  and M 
           j = j + 1
           X = unif(1, 6) // uniform law
           If (X even)
                M = M + 1 // increase in amount
           Otherwise
                M = M − 1 // decrease in amount
           End if
      EndWhile
      S = S + j // accumulation of durations
EndFor
R = S/N // average of game durations
The function unif (1,6) generates a random integer between 1 and 6,
according to a uniform distribution. To implement the simulation, the
algorithm must be translated into a programming (see below). It is
advisable to use a sufficiently large number N, for example, at least
10,000, to obtain reliable results. Below is an example of Python code
to implement this simulation, with different executions varying the
value of N.
import random
# Entering the number of games to play / launch
N = int(input("Enter the value of N = ")) # We use the 
variable S to calculate the accumulation of durations j 
obtained in the games

S = 0
for i in range(1, N + 1):
    j = 0
    M = 1 # Initial availability, here 1 euro
    while M > 0 and M < 5:
        j += 1
        X = random.randint(1, 6) # Generating a random 
number between
        1 and 6
          if X % 2 == 0: M += 1 # Increase in amount
          else: M −= 1 # Decrease in amount
          S += j # We calculate the accumulation of 
durations
          R = S / N # We calculate the average of the 
durations
# Displaying the game average result
print("The average duration of the game (in number of dice 
rolls) = ", R)
With N = 10,000, the following results were obtained from three
executions:
For execution 1:
Entering N = 10,000
Average game duration (in number of dice rolls) = 4.0174
For execution 2:
Entering N = 10,000
Average game duration (in number of dice rolls) = 4.0677
For execution 3:
Entering N = 10,000
Average game duration (in number of dice rolls) = 4.0131
In the same way, if we wish to increase the number N, by setting N =
106, we obtain the following results:
For execution 1:
Entering N = 1,000,000
Average game duration (in number of dice rolls) = 4.004777

For execution 2:
Entering N = 1,000,000
Average game duration (in number of dice rolls) = 4.005863
For execution 2:
Entering N = 1,000,000
Average game duration (in number of dice rolls) = 4.005863
From the two values associated with N (N = 104 and N = 106), we can
note a better approximation for the largest value of N.
The examples provided above illustrate several characteristics of numerical
simulation:
a. A computer is used for its practicality in performing calculations and,
more importantly, for avoiding real experiments. In all examples,
random numbers are generated, and favorable and possible cases are
counted.
b. Statistical frequency is approximated to probability when the
conditions of the law of large numbers are met.
c. It becomes evident that obtaining significant results requires a large
number of experiments (and averaging the results). This approach
aligns with the concept of statistical frequency approaching probability
(as supported by the law of large numbers) and justifies the use of a
computer for handling repetitive tasks.
d. Generating random numbers with a computer, which is inherently
deterministic, presents a challenge. This case will be examined in the
next section.
The process of generating random numbers to simulate events is commonly
referred to as the Monte Carlo method, named after the casino in Monte
Carlo city.

11.5.3. Generation of random numbers
As showed in the previous examples, simulation relies on the use of random
numbers to generate artificial samples.
Before the advent of computers, and even today, tables of random numbers
derived from physical phenomena governed by chance, such as radioactive
decay, were used. A well-known example from this era is a table produced
by the Rand Corporation in 1955. This table was generated by observing the
results of an electronic device functioning similarly a casino roulette. These
tables were used to extract sequences of random numbers. For instance,
consider the Rand Corporation table containing 100,000 digits:
5836093758731238530596885126543129874560985432578904326872109
5010285730265742892
These numbers can then be grouped into pairs to obtain random numbers
ranging from 00 to 99:
58 36 09 37 58 73 12 38 53 05 96 88 51 26 54 31 29 87 45 60 98 54 32 57
89 04 32 68 72 10 95 01 02 85 73 02 65 74 28 92
Alternatively, to obtain random numbers ranging from 000 to 999, the
numbers can be grouped into triples:
583 609 375 873 123 853 059 688 512 654 312 987 456 098 543 257 890
432 687 210 950 102 857 302 657 428
In 1939, Kendall and Babington-Smith created a random number table by
photographing the numbers on a roulette wheel.
Today, computers are primarily used to generate random numbers, although
these are actually pseudo-random, as they are computed rather than truly
random. Programming languages provide functions to generate pseudo-
random numbers. For example, in JavaScript, the Math.random() function
returns a number between 0 (inclusive) and 1 (exclusive). To obtain a
number between a and b (b > a), the following expression can be used: (b –
a) * Math. random() + a. In PHP, the rand() function generates an integer
between 0 and getrandmax() by default. By providing arguments, such as
rand(a, b), it returns an integer between a and b (inclusive).

There are several methods for generating these pseudo-random numbers.
All of these methods produce numbers following a uniform distribution. Let
us explore two of these methods, among many others, and also a third, less
conventional one.
Mid-square process
This process involves choosing an initial number u0 composed of 2m digits.
The number is squared, resulting in a number with 4m digits. From this
result, the 2m central digits are extracted to obtain the number u1. The
process is then repeated with u1 to get u2, etc. Here is an example
illustrating this process:
This generates the following sequence: 763732374781857959929040 ….
Fibonacci process
Start with three numbers u0, u1 and k, where k is larger than the first two
numbers. Generate a sequence of random numbers using the following
procedure:
For example, let u0 = 3,765, u1 = 1,529, and k = 1,6245. The calculations
proceed as follows:

Next, eliminate the first and last digit of each result, yielding the following
sequence:
376152529682211269481126….
Decimal places of π
The irrationality of π implies that the sequence of its decimal places shows
no periodicity and appears completely disordered. While this suggests that
this sequence of decimal places could be considered a sequence of random
numbers, proving this rigorously remains a challenge. Nonetheless, this
sequence is often used in simulations and computations However, in
practice, computers typically do not use this sequence directly.
As of now, 62,800 billion decimal places of π are known. Here are the first
100 of these known decimal places.

11.6. Generating programs
Generating random numbers that follow a specific probability distribution is
essential for conducting simulations. These random numbers will form
samples for the simulation. Initially, consider common probability
distributions, where the cumulative distribution function is particularly
useful. Next, address the general case for any probability distribution. The
importance of the distribution function can be illustrated for both discrete
and continuous variables (see Figure 11.19).

Figure 11.19. Using the distribution function.
The method is as follows:
1. Uniformly choose a number z between 0 and 1.
2. Use the distribution function F(x) to determine the number α that
follows the corresponding probability law (i.e. such that F(α) = z).
This approach allows for the generation of random samples that conform to
the desired probability distribution. The key problem is in inverting the
function F(x), since α is obtained from α = F−1(z).
11.6.1. Usual laws
Previously, it was demonstrated how computers can generate random
numbers (in fact, pseudo-random numbers) following a uniform
distribution. This will serve as the basis for generating random numbers
according to other probability laws.

11.6.2. Any probability law
Consider a practical situation where the probability distribution is unknown,
but statistical data is available. Imagine a counter where customers arrive,
and measurements of the number of arriving customers are taken every 15
minutes. After conducting 1,000 measurements, the resulting frequencies of
customer arrivals are displayed in Figure 11.20. These frequencies are
provided to two decimal places.
Figure 11.20. Any probability law
Assume that this statistic approximates the probability distribution of
customer arrivals, which is unknown. The average number of arrivals is λ =
4.77 arrivals per quarter hour. Suppose that measurements were also taken
of the number of customers served per quarter hour. A total of 100
measurements provide the statistical results (table) shown in Figure 11.21.
Figure 11.21. Any probability law
Assume that this statistic represents the probability distribution of services
(or departures). The average number of services is m = 4.91 per quarter
hour. To study this waiting system and estimate, through simulation, the
average queue length of customers waiting to be served, complete the
previous two tables by associating a range of two-digit numbers with each
frequency, as shown in Figure 11.22. The size of each range should be

proportional to the frequency. For instance, for an arrival count of 3 with a
frequency of 0.23, multiplying by 100 gives an integer of 23. Given that 12
was the last value for the previous arrival count, create a range of 13
numbers from 13 to 35.
Figure 11.22. Using number ranges

Sequence S1: 90 27 80 39 52 98 24 79 …
Sequence S2: 72 71 67 53 43 97 30 98 …
Figure 11.23. Arrivals and departures table
For the first quarter-hour, draw 90 for arrivals and 72 for departures.
According to the arrivals table, the number 90 corresponds to eight
arrivals table, while 72 corresponds six departures.

For the second quarter-hour, draw 27 for arrivals and 71 for departures.

corresponds to six departures.
Continuing in this manner, create a table showing, arrivals and
departures for each quarter-hour, as shown in Figure 11.23.
In this table, by calculating the difference between the number of arrivals
and the number of services (i.e. departures), the length of the queue is
obtained. If the difference is positive, it represents the length of the queue;
if negative, the queue length is zero.
With a sufficiently large number of values for S1 and S2 (i.e. a large sample
size), the average queue lengths can be determined, providing the desired
result. To ensure a reliable result, consider the following precautions:
1. Number of periods: ensure that there are a sufficiently large number of
periods, at least 100. It is also recommended to repeat the simulation
multiple times and average the results to obtain a more accurate
estimate.
2. Edge effects: in the given example, the waiting system is not “open-
ended”. For instance, the service only during specific hours, such as an
8-hour workday. It is therefore important to account for “edge” effects.
To address these, discard results from the beginning and end of the
simulation period retaining only those that reflect the system’s stable,
or “steady-state”, regime.
11.7. M/M/1 waiting system
Waiting phenomena are prevalent in daily life (such as waiting at counters
or experiencing traffic jams), and they significantly impact economic, social
and financial aspects. Waiting systems are studied through various models
that share certain common characteristics.
A waiting system typically consists of a queue where customers wait and
one or more stations that perform the required services (see Figure 11.24).

Figure 11.24. M/M/1 waiting system
In a queuing system, individuals enter the queue from a source, which can
be either infinite (open systems) or finite (closed systems). They advance in
line according to a specific order of priority and are directed to a station
where they receive individual service. Once the service is complete, they
leave the station. The arrival and departure processes are governed by
probabilistic laws, which may differ from one another.
The interesting case is that of equilibrium, where the queue neither grows
indefinitely nor remains perpetually empty. The waiting system is subject to
a process of arrivals (births) and departures (deaths), and understanding this
balance is crucial for comprehending the overall functioning of the system.
Two key parameters, which are often random, influence the behavior of the
system over time:
Arrival rate: the frequency (or rate) of arrival of individuals.
Service duration: the duration of service.
Arrival frequency represents the rate at which individuals enter the waiting
system. It may vary depending on various factors such as time of day, day
of the week or other relevant variables. Duration of service refers to the
time required to serve each individual before they leave the station. This
duration may also be subject to random variations.
One of the simplest waiting systems is the M/M/1 system. The first “M”
indicates the exponential distribution of arrival times, meaning the time
intervals between consecutive arrivals (which therefore follow an
exponential distribution). The second “M” signifies that the duration of
services also follows an exponential distribution. The number “1” indicates

that there is a single service station, which implies the existence of a single
queue.
Additionally, it is assumed that the waiting system is open, meaning that
there is a continuous flow of arrivals. The priority rule in the queue is FIFO
(First-In, First-Out): individuals are served in the queue according to the
order of their arrival.
It is important to remember that if a variable follows an exponential
distribution, its inverse follows a Poisson distribution. In other words, if the
time between two consecutive arrivals follows an exponential distribution
with a mean of , then the frequency of arrivals follows a Poisson
distribution with a mean λ. Similarly, if the duration of a service follows an
exponential distribution with a mean of  then the frequency of departures
or services follows a Poisson distribution with a mean of μ.
Thus, the M/M/1 system can be analyzed from two equivalent perspectives,
as shown in Figure 11.25.
Figure 11.25. Two equivalent ways of seeing a waiting system
11.7.1. Theoretical study
Consider the time interval from t to t + h, where h is a very small positive
duration (time). It can be hypothesized that during this interval h, at most
one arrival or departure occurs (the “rare events” hypothesis). Therefore, if

there are n individuals in the system at t + h, there are only four possible
scenarios for the system at time t:
n individuals at time t: no arrivals and no departures or one arrival and
one departure during the duration (interval) h.
n − 1 individuals at time t: one arrival during the interval h.
n + 1 individuals at time t: one departure during the interval h.
Let pn(t) denote the probability of having n individuals in the system at time
t. To describe the evolution of the system at time t + h, the compound
probabilities can be used
where ρn,m(h) represents the probability of transitioning from a state with n
individuals to a state with m individuals during the interval h. This
probability expression accounts for all three possible cases. The process is
represented as a Markov chain where the states are the number of
individuals in the waiting system (see Figure 11.26).
Figure 11.26. Markov process
The probability ρn,n(h) is approximately greater as h becomes larger. This
leads to the following approximations:
Since the sum of the transition probabilities must be equal to 1, it follows
that:

where O(h) represents terms that become negligible (tend to 0) as h
approaches 0. The parameter λn is the “birth rate” and generally depends on
the current state n. For simplicity, λn is assumed to be constant (so λn = λ).
Similarly, μn is the death rate also assumed to be constant (so μn= μ).
With these notations, the probability pn(t + h) for n > 0 is expressed as:
Rearranging and dividing by h gives:
Taking the limit as h approaches 0, the term O(h) becomes negligible, and
the equation simplifies to:
The primary focus is on the equilibrium state, where the system reaches a
steady-state. In this case, the probabilities associated with the different
states of the system no longer depend on time:

instance (see example 11.15.), if pk(t) represents the probability of having k

The parameter λ therefore represents the mean arrival rate, i.e. the mean
number of arrivals per unit of time. Similarly, μ represents the mean
departure rate.

service.
The assumption that λ < μ is justified because, for the queue to remain
stable and not grow indefinitely, the arrival rate must be less than the
departure rate. Thus, on average, λ < μ.

EXAMPLE 11.16.–
A bank office in a small town has only one teller. The arrival rate is
three customers per hour. The average service duration is 15 minutes (or
¼ hour). The time between two consecutive arrivals follows an
exponential distribution with parameter λ (hence, the average time
between arrivals is 
), and the service duration follows an exponential
distribution with parameter μ (hence, the average service time is 
).
This represents an M/M/1 waiting system with λ = 3 and μ = 4. The
waiting system is “viable” because λ < μ or Ψ < 1.
Theoretical results indicate the following:
The average number of customers in the bank is 
 = 3
customers.
The average number of customers in the queue is 
 = 2.25
customers.
The average time spent by a customer in the bank is 
 = 1
hour.
The average time spent by a customer in the queue is 
hour = 45 minutes.
It is verified that ts – tF = 15 minutes.
11.7.2. Simulation
The basic data are the average arrival rate λ and the average departure (or
service) rate μ, assuming an M/M/1 system. Figure 11.27 describes a typical
situation with nine elements. N individuals, numbered 1, 2, 3, .... N, are
successively entered into the waiting system. For each individual k > 1, the
following are defined or calculated:

The time difference between two successive arrivals, I[k] is generated
randomly according to an exponential law with parameter λ.
The arrival date, da[k], is calculated as the sum of the previous
individual’s arrival date, da[k − 1], and I[k]. For the first individual,
da[1] is set to 0. Thus, da[k] = da[k − 1] + I[k], and for k = 1, da[1] =
0.
Figure 11.27. nS, nF: number of individuals in the system and in the
queue
The duration of the service, D[k], is randomly generated according to
an exponential law with parameter μ.
The start date of service, ds[k], determined by taking the greater value
between the departure date of the previous individual, dd[k − 1], and
the individual’s arrival date k, da[k]. If da[k] > dd[k − 1], the station is
free, and the service can start immediately. If da[k] < dd[k − 1], service
will begin only after the departure of the (k − 1)th individual. In other
words, ds[k] = max{dd[k − 1],da[k]}, where dd[k − 1] is the departure
date of the (k − 1)th individual.
The departure date, dd[k], is calculated by adding the duration of
service, D[k], to the service start date, ds[k]. In other words, dd[k] =
ds[k] + D[k].
The time spent by the individual k in the queue, tF[k], is calculated as
the difference between the service start date, ds[k], and the arrival date,

da[k]. In other words, tF[k] = ds[k] − da[k].
The time spent by the individual k in the waiting system, tS[k], is
calculated as the difference between the departure date, dd[k], and the
arrival date, da[k]. In other words, tS[k] = dd[k] − da[k].
For the first individual, who is first to enter the waiting system, the initial
values are da[1] = 0, ds[1] = 0, dd[1] = ds[1] + D[1], tF[1] = 0, and tS[1] =
dd[1] − da[1].
From the tables tF[k] and tS[k], the average values tF and tS can be
calculated as follows:
Summarize the algorithm that handles the timing of events and the
calculation of tF and tS:
ttf=0 // total queue time
tts=0 // total system waiting time
For k = 1 to N
    If k = 1
           I[1]=0
           da[1]=0
           ds[1]=0
           D[1]=genexp(m)
           dd[1]=D[1]
           tf[1]=0 // wait time
           tS[1]=dd[1]=da[1] // presence time in the system
           ttf=ttf+tf[1]
           tts = tts + ts[1]
    Otherwise
           I[k]=genexp(l)
           da[k]=da[k-1]+I[k]
           ds[k]= max{dd[k-1], da[k]}
           D[k]=genexp(m)
           dd[k]=ds[k]+D[k]
           tf[k]=ds[k]-da[k] // wait time
           tS[k]=dd[k]-da[k] // presence time in the system
           ttf=ttf+tf[k]
           tts = tts + ts[k]

    End if
EndFor
In this algorithm, tF and tS represent the average waiting time in the queue
and the average presence time spent in the system, respectively.
The algorithm uses the function genexp(x), which generates a random
number following the exponential distribution:
where z = unif(0,1). The function unif(0,1). returns a random number
uniformly distributed between 0 and 1.
Calculating the average number nF of individuals in the queue and the
average number nS of individuals in the waiting system is less
straightforward. This is because the durations during which there are values
for the number of individuals in the queue and in the system need to be
considered such as durations 2, 1, 1 and 3, as shown in Figure 11.27. The
key dates are provided in the tables da[k],dd[k] and ds[k].
These three lists can be merged while maintaining chronological order and
noting whether each date corresponds to an arrival, a, a start of service, s, or
a departure, d. Multiple events can occur at the same date (e.g. date 12 in
Figure 11.27, which represents an arrival, a start of service and a departure).
In such cases, events are processed in the order: arrival (da), departure (dd)
and start of service (ds). Identical dates should be included and considered
as separate events.
For example, from Figure 11.27, the tables in Figure 11.28 show sorted
dates from da, ds and dd, with the corresponding events listed in line E.
To determine the average number of intervals, represent the consecutive
time intervals with their length, starting and ending events, as shown in
Figure 11.29. In these tables, a, s and d denote an arrival, a start of service
and a departure, respectively. Note that there are intervals of zero length
corresponding to identical dates of different events.

Figure 11.28. Timelines
To determine the number nS of individuals present in the system during an
interval, add 1 each time an interval begins with an arrival and subtract 1
each time an interval begins with a departure.
Figure 11.29. Consecutive time intervals
Figure 11.30. Completed tables
Likewise, to determine the number of individuals nF in the queue during an
interval, add 1 each time an interval begins with an arrival and subtract 1
each time an interval begins with a start of service. Figure 11.30 shows the
completed tables.
The weighted averages are then calculated using the following formulas:

where i denotes an interval, d[i] is its duration, nS[i] is the number of
individuals in the system, and nF[i] is the number of individuals in the
queue.
The algorithm below follows the one given above, with the tables da,dd,
and ds now being known.
L = concatenation of lists da, dd, ds
For k=1 to N
    Le[k]="a"
    Le[k+N]="d"
    Le[k+2*N]="s"
EndFor
//LL= sorted list of L according to the values of L[h] for h=1 
to 3*N
//LLe = sorted list of LLe in the same order as LL
// list of time intervals
i = 0 // number of intervals between two consecutive events
nS=0 // number of individuals present in interval i
nF=0 // number of individuals waiting in interval i
T[i][1]=0 // low value of the 1st interval
T[i][2]=0 // high value of the 1st interval
T[i][3]=0 // duration of the 1st interval
T[i][4]="a" // event of the start of the 1st interval
T[i][5]="s" // end of 1st interval event
T[i][6]=0//number of individuals in system during the i 
interval
T[i][7]=0 //number of individuals waiting during the interval i
For h = 1 to 3*N-1
    i=i+1 // interval number
    T[i][1]=LL[h] // low value of the interval
    T[i][2]=LL[h+1] // high value of the interval
    T[i][3]=T[i][h+1]-T[i][h] //interval duration
    T[i][4]=LLe[h] // event for low value
    T[i][5]=LLe[h+1] // event for the high value
    If T[i][4]="a" // an individual enters the system
           nS=nS+1
           nF=nF+1
    End if
    T[i][6]=nS // number of presents during the interval
    T[i][7]=nF // number of patients during the interval If

    T[i][4]="d" // an individual leaves the system
           nS=nS-1
    End if
    T[i][6]=nS
    If T[i][4]="s" // an individual leaves the queue
           nF=nF-1
    End if
    T[i][7]=nF
EndFor
M=i
//calculation of averages
TnS = 0
TnF=0
For i=1 to M
  TnS=TnS+T[i][6]*T[i][3]//cumulative products 
duration×individuals presents
  TnF=TnF+T[i][7]*T[i][3]//cumulative products 
duration×individuals waiting
EndFor
mnS=TnS/LL[3*N] // average number of individuals present in the 
system
mnF=TnF/LL[3*N] // average number of individuals waiting

EXAMPLE 11.17.–
Concretely, by using a program based on the previously described
algorithms and repeating the experiment 20 times with 110 individuals
in an M/M/1 type waiting system, with an arrival rate of λ = 3 arrivals
per unit time and a service rate of μ = 4 services per unit time, the
results are similar to those presented in the table in Figure 11.31.
To ensure a steady state, the first five and last five individuals are
eliminated, resulting in a sample of 100 individuals.
The benefit of conducting a large number of experiments (potentially
more than 20) is evident, as results can vary significantly between
experiments. The primary interest is in the average values obtained.
Comparing these values to the theoretical values for an M/M/1 queue,
as given in example 11.16 (tS = 1, tF = 0.75, nS = 3 and nF =2.25),
shows that the obtained values are relatively close, with for tS and tF
aligning more closely than ns and nF.
Calculating the standard deviations σtS, σtF, σnS, and σnF, from the table

11.8.3. Appendix3: Button’s needle
The probability that the needle will pass through a gap between the slats
needs to be calculated. Assuming the parquet floor is perfect; the impact
location is irrelevant since all the parquet slats have equivalent properties.
Thus, the problem can be approached using two random variables.
Considering horizontal slats and focusing on a specific slat (see Figure
11.32), the calculation proceeds as follows:
The distance y of the needle’s lowest point from the upper dividing
line is the first random variable, taking values between 0 and d.
The angle θ of the needle relative to the horizontal is the second
random variable, taking values between 0 and π.
Figure 11.32. Geometry of the problem
It is straightforward to determine that the needle crosses the upper
separation if y < L sin(θ). This relationship can be shown graphically:
Figure 11.33(a) shows the various possible points (light gray rectangle)
when y and θ are chosen randomly, assuming the selection follows a
uniform probability distribution.

[11.1]
Figure 11.33. Different scenarios
Case (d > L) (see Figure 11.33(b)):
The curve y < L sin(θ) forms a sinusoid arc, and the region below this curve
(shown in dark gray) represents the situations where the needle intersects
the separation. The probability of this occurrence is the ratio of the area of
the dark gray region to the area of the rectangle (with part of the rectangle
covered by the dark gray region). The area of the rectangle is (dπ); the area
of the dark gray portion is given by:
Thus, the probability is:
Case (d < L) (see Figure 11.33(c)):

probability can be estimated using the law of large numbers approximating
the probability to the statistical frequency (f = n/N ≈ p):
